Then ten lepers, all cleaned up on their way to the priests, and exactly one comes back to say thanks. Luke saves the punchline for the end of the sentence: he was a Samaritan. Asked when the kingdom shows up, he says it doesn't come with a timetable and it's already among them. Then Noah and Lot, cited not for wickedness but for how ordinary the day looked: eating, buying, building, right up to the second. Don't go back for your stuff. Remember Lot's wife.
LUK 18:1-43	The persistence lesson runs on a judge who openly admits he doesn't fear God or care about people and just wants the widow to stop showing up. The humility lesson runs on a Pharisee whose prayer is a list of everyone else's flaws, and a tax collector who manages one line: God, be merciful, I'm a sinner. Babies get brought over, shooed off, then let through. A rich ruler with a spotless record asks what he's missing, hears "sell everything," and walks off sad. Camel, needle, and the crowd asking who's left. Then the third death prediction, spitting and flogging included, and Luke says flatly they understood none of it. At Jericho a blind man out-shouts the people telling him to be quiet, which is exactly how he gets asked what he wants.
LUK 19:1-23	Zacchaeus is a senior tax collector, rich, and Luke makes a point of saying he's short, so he can't see over anybody and ends up in a tree. Jesus stops right underneath and invites himself to dinner. The crowd grumbles about the company he keeps. Zacchaeus answers with numbers: half of everything to the poor, and quadruple back to anyone he shook down. Then, because Jerusalem is close and everyone thinks the kingdom is landing this week, a parable: a man goes abroad to be made king, while his own citizens send a petition saying they don't want him. Ten servants get a mina each. He comes back and audits them. Ten more, five more, and then the guy who kept his coin in a hanky and has opinions about the boss. Next time on Luke: the reckoning.
LUK 19:24-48	The coin gets handed to the guy who already has ten, and the citizens who didn't want him as king are executed in front of him. Luke does not soften that. Then the entry into Jerusalem: a colt nobody's ever ridden, borrowed with a line prepared in advance, cloaks on the road, and the disciples yelling about everything they've watched him do. Told to shut them up, he says the stones would take over. And then, in the middle of his own parade, he stops and cries, and what he describes is a siege: barricades, encirclement, children on the ground, not one stone left standing. He clears the temple, then just teaches there every day, which leaves the chief priests wanting him dead and unable to touch him, because the crowd won't stop listening.
LUK 20:1-47	They demand his credentials, and he asks about John's baptism instead. Luke lets us listen to them work it out: say heaven and we look like idiots, say men and the crowd stones us. So they go with "no idea," and he says great, then no answer for you either. Then the vineyard: three servants beaten, the owner's son murdered, and the crowd gasping "may that never happen," which only makes it land harder. Then spies open with compliments and ask about Caesar's tax, and get handed a coin. Then the Sadducees with their seven-brothers scenario, answered with angels and, better, with Moses at the burning bush. A scribe actually compliments him, and the questions stop. He asks his own about David, then describes the scribes out loud: long robes, best seats, long prayers, widows' houses eaten.
LUK 21:1-38	Opens with a widow dropping in two tiny coins, which he says outweighs every large donation in the room, because they gave spare change and she gave everything. Then people start admiring the temple masonry, and he tells them it's all coming down. Asked when, he warns about impostors and says wars aren't the finale. And notice the order: the arrests come first. Synagogues, prisons, kings, governors, and don't rehearse a speech, the words will show up. Your own family will turn you in. Some of you will die. Also, not a hair of your head will perish, and he lets those two sit side by side. Armies around Jerusalem means run, and he says plainly how brutal it'll be for pregnant and nursing women. Then sun, moon, stars, and heads up, not down.
LUK 22:1-36	Luke says outright that Satan got into Judas, and off Judas goes to name a price. Setting up the meal is weirdly covert: follow the man carrying a water jar, give the householder a set phrase, take the upstairs room. At the table: a cup, then bread as his body, then a cup as the new covenant, then the flat line that his betrayer's hand is on the table right now. They start guessing who, then drop it and start arguing about which of them is the greatest, at that meal, and get told the greatest one serves. Simon hears he's about to be sifted like wheat, says he's ready to die, and is told he has until the rooster. Then the odd instruction: sell your cloak, buy a sword. Next time on Luke: the Mount of Olives.
LUK 22:37-71	They produce two swords and he says "that's enough," which is not encouragement. On the Mount of Olives he prays a stone's throw off, asks for the cup, then defers, and Luke alone reports the angel and sweat like drops of blood. The disciples are asleep, and Luke says it was grief, not laziness. Judas walks up at the front of a crowd, and Jesus names the kiss before it happens. Somebody takes off the high priest's servant's right ear, and only in Luke does it go back on. He asks why they needed weapons for a man who taught in the temple every day. Then the courtyard, three denials spread over an hour, the rooster, and the detail only Luke has: Jesus turns and looks at Peter. Then the blindfold and the beating.
LUK 23:1-28	The charges they bring Pilate are political, because blasphemy won't fly with Rome: tax refusal and calling himself a king. Pilate finds nothing, hears the word Galilee, and immediately ships the problem to Herod. Herod's thrilled, hoping for a miracle, gets total silence, mocks him, dresses him in fancy clothes and sends him back. Luke notes that the two of them became friends that day. Pilate says twice that neither he nor Herod found a capital offence and offers a flogging as a compromise. The crowd asks for Barabbas instead, a man jailed for insurrection and murder, and Luke says their voices prevailed, which is a verdict decided by volume. Simon of Cyrene gets pulled off the road to carry the cross, and the weeping women are told to weep for themselves. Next time on Luke: the Skull.
LUK 23:29-56	What he tells the mourning women is about their future, not his. At the Skull he's crucified between two criminals, and the first thing out of his mouth is a request that they be forgiven, because they don't know what they're doing. The mockery comes in layers: the rulers, then the soldiers, then one of the men dying next to him. And it's the other criminal who says the only accurate thing anyone says all day: this man did nothing wrong. He's promised Paradise that same day. Then darkness over the land, the temple curtain torn, and a last line spoken to the Father. Luke's centurion says "righteous man," not Son of God, and the crowd walks home hitting their chests. Joseph, a council member who voted against all of it, gives up the tomb.
LUK 24:1-26	The women turn up at dawn with burial spices for a body that isn't there, and instead of an announcement they get a question: why are you looking for the living among the dead? They're told to remember what he said back in Galilee, and they do. Luke names them, then reports that the apostles wrote it off as nonsense, which he does not soften. Peter runs to the tomb anyway, finds the linen lying there by itself, and goes home confused. Then the Emmaus road, where two disciples explain the crucifixion to the crucified guy, in detail, including their own dashed hopes and the women's report they haven't believed. He calls them slow, which is fair, and starts working through the prophets. Next time on Luke: the bread.
LUK 24:27-53	He walks them through Moses and the prophets the whole way, then acts like he's moving on, and they talk him into staying for the night. He breaks the bread at the table, and that's the moment they recognise him, right before he vanishes. They admit their hearts had been burning the entire walk. Then they hike straight back to Jerusalem and find the eleven already saying he's appeared to Simon. Jesus turns up in the room, and they assume ghost, so he shows them his hands and feet and eats a piece of grilled fish in front of them to settle it. Then the brief: everything written had to happen, repentance and forgiveness go to every nation starting here, you're the witnesses, and wait in the city for power. At Bethany he blesses them and is taken up.
MAT 1:1-25	Forty-two rounds of who-fathered-who, sorted into three neat batches of fourteen, and the family tree quietly includes Tamar, Rahab, Ruth and the woman Matthew will only call Uriah's wife. Then the tidiness breaks. Mary's pregnant, Joseph knows it isn't his, and he decides to end it quietly rather than publicly. Angel turns up in a dream: marry her, the kid is from the Holy Spirit, name him Jesus. Joseph wakes up and just does it.
MAT 2:1-23	Star-watchers from the east stroll into Jerusalem and ask the current king where the new king is. Herod goes pale, pulls in the scholars, gets Bethlehem out of them, and asks the visitors to send word so he can worship too. Nobody should have believed that. The star parks over the house, they hand over gold, frankincense and myrrh, and a dream tells them to take the long way home. Joseph gets his own dream and runs for Egypt. Herod, humiliated, kills every boy in Bethlehem under two. The family only comes back after he's dead, and settles in Nazareth.
MAT 3:1-17	John's out in the desert in camel hair eating bugs and honey, and somehow the entire Jordan region shows up to get dunked. Pharisees and Sadducees arrive and he opens with "you offspring of vipers." Being Abraham's kids counts for nothing. God can make more Abrahams out of rocks. There's already an ax at the roots. Then the guy he's been hyping, the one whose sandals he says he isn't fit to carry, gets in line for baptism, and John says this is backwards. Jesus insists. Sky opens, dove drops, voice says: my Son, and I'm pleased.
MAT 4:1-25	Forty days, no food, and here comes the devil with three offers: turn the rocks into bread, jump off the temple and let the angels catch you, or bow once and own every kingdom on earth. He even quotes scripture for the jump. Jesus answers all three with scripture and that's that. Devil leaves, angels bring food. John gets arrested, so Jesus relocates to Capernaum and starts preaching John's exact line. He calls two sets of brothers off their boats and they drop everything, including their dad. Crowds start arriving from everywhere.
MAT 5:1-24	Up the hill, sits down, and starts handing out congratulations to everyone who looks like they're losing: the broke in spirit, the grieving, the gentle, the ones starving for justice, the merciful, the clear-hearted, the peacemakers, and anyone getting trashed on his account. You're salt, don't go bland. You're light, so get off the floor and onto the stand. And no, he's not scrapping the law, not one pen stroke. The bar goes up instead: calling your brother a fool lands in the same file as murder, and a grudge means you leave your offering at the altar and go fix it first. Next time on Matthew: it gets harder.
MAT 5:25-48	The bar keeps climbing. Settle with the guy suing you before a judge gets involved. Staring at someone with intent already counts. An eye or a hand is a cheaper loss than all of you. Divorce gets narrowed to one exception. Oaths: just quit, you can't even change your own hair color, so say yes or no and mean it. Somebody hits you, offer the other cheek. Sued for your coat, hand over the cloak too. Forced one mile, walk two. Love your enemies, because God's sun comes up on everybody, and loving people who already like you is a trick tax collectors can manage.
MAT 6:1-34	Three ways to be religious for an audience get shredded: giving with a trumpet, praying loudly on the corner, fasting with a face you wrecked on purpose. All three already got paid, in attention. Prayer isn't a word count, so here's a short one, plus the uncomfortable part about forgiving people if you want forgiving. Earth treasure gets eaten by moths or stolen, and your heart follows your money. You cannot work for God and cash both. Then: quit panicking. Birds don't farm. Wildflowers dress better than Solomon. Worry buys you zero extra minutes. Tomorrow can handle itself.
MAT 7:1-29	Judge people and the same ruler comes back for you. Deal with the plank in your own eye before you go tweezing specks out of anyone else's. Don't throw pearls at pigs. Ask, seek, knock, because no dad hands his kid a rock when the kid asked for bread. Treat people how you want to be treated, and that's the whole law. The good gate is narrow and barely used. Fake prophets come dressed as sheep, so check the fruit. And "Lord, Lord, look at all the miracles I did" gets "I never knew you." Two houses, one storm, one foundation that holds. Crowd's stunned. He doesn't talk like the scribes.
MAT 8:1-34	Off the mountain, a leper asks, and Jesus actually touches him, then tells him to go see the priest and keep it quiet. A Roman officer asks for a remote healing using chain-of-command logic and gets called the best faith in Israel. Peter's mother-in-law gets healed and immediately starts serving food. Two would-be followers get cold water: no pillow for the Son of Man, and let the dead bury the dead. Then he naps through a storm, gets shaken awake by actual fishermen, calls them low on faith, and tells the weather to stop. Across the lake, two demoniacs; the demons ask to relocate into pigs, the pigs run off a cliff, and the town politely asks him to go.
MAT 9:1-38	A paralyzed guy shows up on a bed and gets his sins forgiven, which the scribes decide is blasphemy without saying it out loud. So Jesus heals him too, basically to prove the point. Then he hires a tax collector, eats with that whole crowd, and tells the Pharisees to go look up "mercy, not sacrifice." Asked why his people don't fast: nobody fasts at a wedding, and you don't patch old clothes with new cloth. Then a dead girl's dad interrupts, a woman sick twelve years grabs his coat and is healed, and the paid mourners laugh at him right before he raises the girl. Two blind men healed, told to keep it quiet, immediately don't.
MAT 10:1-42	Twelve guys get named and handed power over demons and disease, and Matthew tags Judas as the traitor right there in the roster. Packing list: nothing. No cash, no bag, no spare shirt, no sandals, no walking stick. Town won't listen? Shake the dust off and go. Then the honest part: courts, floggings, governors, kings, and family members turning you in. Don't rehearse a speech, the words will show up. Don't be scared of anyone who can only kill your body. Sparrows cost pennies and God still counts them, and your hairs are numbered too. He says he brought a sword, not peace, and that loving your parents more than him doesn't work. Even a cup of cold water counts.
MAT 11:1-30	John's in prison and sends a message asking, basically, did I bet on the right guy. Jesus doesn't say yes. He sends back a list: blind see, lame walk, lepers clean, deaf hear, dead up, poor getting good news. Then he turns to the crowd and talks up the guy who just doubted him: more than a prophet, the promised messenger, Elijah if you'll take it. This generation, he says, is like kids who won't play any game you start. John didn't eat, so he's demon-possessed. Jesus eats, so he's a drunk. Chorazin, Bethsaida and Capernaum get told Tyre, Sidon and Sodom would've done better. Then the tone flips completely: come here if you're worn out, my yoke is easy.
MAT 12:1-28	Disciples grab a snack in a grain field on the Sabbath and the Pharisees write it up. Jesus counters with David eating the holy bread and with priests who technically work every Sabbath. Also: Lord of the Sabbath, thanks. Next scene, a guy with a withered hand and a loaded question about Sabbath healing. Jesus asks who wouldn't pull their sheep out of a pit on a Saturday, then fixes the hand. The Pharisees walk out and start planning a murder. He leaves, heals everybody anyway, and tells them to keep quiet about it. Then he frees a blind mute man, and the official explanation is: Satan helped. Next time on Matthew: he demolishes that.
MAT 12:29-50	Point one: you can't loot a strong guy's house without tying him up first. Point two: every sin is forgivable except talking against the Holy Spirit, which isn't. The tree shows in the fruit, your mouth leaks whatever your heart's full of, and yes, the throwaway comments count. They demand a sign, and he gives them Jonah: three days in the fish, three days in the ground, and by the way Nineveh repented for a worse preacher than me. Then the creepy bit: a demon leaves, finds the house swept and empty, and moves back in with seven worse. Someone says his mom and brothers are outside. He points at his disciples and says these are my family.
MAT 13:1-30	Crowd's so big he has to teach from a boat. Story one is about soil. Seed hits the path, the rocks, the thorns, and finally decent ground that pays back a hundred, sixty, thirty times. Disciples ask why the riddles, and he says the crowd looks without seeing, quoting Isaiah at them. Then he explains it anyway: snatched, shallow, choked, or actually growing. Story two: somebody sneaks in overnight and plants weeds in the wheat. The workers want to rip them out, the owner says no, you'll take the wheat with them. Let it all grow, sort it at harvest. Next time on Matthew: the sorting, plus one very expensive pearl.
MAT 13:31-58	More comparisons, all small stuff: a mustard seed that turns into a tree birds nest in, and yeast a woman works into three measures of flour. Matthew says he told the crowds nothing but parables. Inside, the disciples want the weeds explained and get a straight decoder: sower, field, good seed, weeds, harvest, angels, furnace. Then two guys who sell literally everything, one for a buried treasure, one for a single pearl. Then a net that hauls in everything and gets sorted on the beach. He goes home and gets the classic hometown treatment: isn't this the carpenter's kid, we know his mother and his brothers. So he barely does anything there.
MAT 14:1-36	Herod hears about Jesus and nervously decides John's come back from the dead. Matthew backs up to explain: John told him the marriage was illegal, so John got locked up, and at Herod's birthday a dance plus a rash oath plus a request in front of guests ended with John beheaded. His disciples bury him and go tell Jesus. Jesus leaves by boat for somewhere empty, the crowd walks around the lake and beats him there, and he heals them anyway. Disciples say send them home to eat. He says you feed them. Five loaves, two fish, five thousand men plus women and kids, twelve baskets left. Then he walks across the lake at night, they scream ghost, and Peter sinks the moment the wind spooks him.
MAT 15:1-39	The Jerusalem crowd shows up to complain that his disciples don't wash their hands. He fires back about their own loophole: call your money a gift to God and you never have to support your parents again. Then quotes Isaiah about lips saying one thing and hearts elsewhere. His rule: your mouth is an exit, not an entrance, and what comes out of the heart is the ugly list. Then a Canaanite woman asks him to help her daughter. He ignores her, says he was sent to Israel, says something about children's bread and dogs, and she comes right back with the crumbs line and wins. Later he feeds four thousand off seven loaves, while the disciples ask where they'd get bread, like the last time didn't happen.
MAT 16:1-28	They want a sign from heaven. He points out they can predict tomorrow's weather off a red sky but can't read the moment they're standing in, and offers Jonah, again. Then he warns them about the yeast of the Pharisees, and the disciples panic about having forgotten bread. He has to walk them through both feedings before they get that yeast means teaching. Then the big question: who do people say I am, and then, who do you say I am. Peter nails it and gets renamed the rock and handed the keys. Ten minutes later Jesus says the Jerusalem trip ends in his death, Peter tells him no it doesn't, and gets called Satan. Terms of employment: carry your cross, and the whole world isn't worth your life.
MAT 17:1-27	Up a mountain, and his face lights up like the sun and his clothes go blinding white. Moses and Elijah show up for a chat. Peter, standing in the wildest moment of his life, suggests putting up tents. A cloud rolls in, a voice says this is my Son, listen to him, and all three hit the dirt. They look up and it's just Jesus. On the way down: don't tell anyone yet, and yes, Elijah already came and got mistreated, which they figure out means John. At the bottom a father says the disciples couldn't help his son. Jesus does it in one line, then tells them mustard-seed faith moves mountains. He predicts his death again and everyone's crushed. Then the temple tax comes up, and the money arrives inside a fish.
MAT 18:1-35	They ask which of them is the greatest, which kind of answers itself, and Jesus parks a kid in the middle of the group. Be like this or you're not getting in. Trip up one of these little ones and you'd be better off in the lake wearing a millstone. Hand, foot, eye causing problems? Lose it. Then: a shepherd ditches ninety-nine sheep to go find one, and is more excited about the one. Got a problem with someone? Talk to them alone, then bring witnesses, then take it to the assembly. Peter tries to cap forgiveness at seven and gets seventy times seven, plus a story about a guy forgiven a fortune who immediately grabs a coworker by the throat over pocket change.
MAT 19:1-30	The trap question: can a man divorce his wife for any reason he likes. He goes back to Genesis instead of the rulebook, says the two become one flesh, and that Moses only allowed divorce because people are hard-hearted. The disciples' takeaway is that marriage sounds like a bad deal, which was not the point. Kids get brought over, the disciples try to shoo them away, and get overruled. Then a guy with a spotless record asks what he's missing and gets told to sell everything. He walks off sad, because he's loaded. Cue the camel and the needle, and the disciples asking who can possibly be saved. Peter asks what they get for leaving everything: twelve thrones, and by the way the order flips.
MAT 20:1-34	A vineyard owner hires workers all day long, dawn shift through the last hour, then pays everyone the same denarius and makes sure the latecomers get paid first, in full view. The dawn crew is furious. He points out they got exactly what they signed for and that being generous isn't stealing. Then, on the road to Jerusalem, Jesus lays out the ending: handed over, condemned, mocked, whipped, crucified, raised on day three. Immediate response? James and John's mom asks for the two best seats. He asks if they can drink his cup, the other ten fume, and he explains that here the boss is the servant. Then two blind guys in Jericho shout over a crowd telling them to shut up, and he stops for them.
MAT 21:1-23	Two disciples get sent to grab a donkey and a colt, which Matthew calls the prophecy about a humble king on a colt. Cloaks and branches hit the road, everyone's yelling Hosanna, and the city is buzzing about who this guy even is. Then the temple, where he throws out the merchants, flips the money changers' tables and the dove sellers' benches, and says this was supposed to be a house of prayer, not a robbers' hideout. He heals people right there. The chief priests complain about kids chanting and get a psalm quoted at them. Next morning he finds a fig tree with leaves and no figs and kills it, then tells the stunned disciples faith moves mountains. Back at the temple the officials demand to know who authorized this. Next time on Matthew: he asks a question first.
MAT 21:24-46	He makes them a deal: tell me if John's baptism came from heaven or from people, and I'll tell you where my authority comes from. Both answers wreck them, so they go with "we don't know," and he says great, then I'm not telling you either. Then a story: two sons, one says no and goes anyway, one says sure and doesn't. Guess which one did the job. Also, tax collectors and prostitutes are getting in ahead of you. Second story: tenants beat, stone and kill the servants sent for the rent, then murder the owner's son figuring the land becomes theirs. He asks what the owner should do, and the priests sentence themselves out loud. Rejected stone, cornerstone, kingdom handed elsewhere. They'd arrest him, but the crowd loves him.
MAT 22:1-46	King throws a wedding, the invited guests can't be bothered, and some of them kill the messengers, so he wipes them out and fills the hall with whoever's on the road. Then a guy shows up dressed wrong and gets thrown out anyway. Next, three traps in a row. The tax question arrives smothered in compliments, and he just asks whose face is on the coin. Then the Sadducees, who don't believe in resurrection, bring a made-up scenario about one woman and seven brothers, and get told they've misread the whole thing. A lawyer asks for the single biggest commandment and gets two, because everything else hangs on them. Then Jesus asks his own question about David calling his son Lord, and nobody asks him anything ever again.
MAT 23:1-39	Do what they teach, don't copy what they do. They write heavy rules and carry none of them, wear extra-wide prayer boxes and long fringes, grab the best seats and love being called Rabbi. You, he says, collect no titles. Then the woes, and he does not hold back: they slam the kingdom door and stand in it, cross oceans to make one convert who ends up worse, invent a whole legal system for which oaths count, tithe their herb garden while skipping justice and mercy, filter out gnats and swallow camels, scrub the outside of the cup, look like gorgeous tombs stuffed with bones, and build monuments to prophets their own ancestors killed. Then the anger drops out completely: Jerusalem, a hen, chicks that wouldn't come, an empty house.
MAT 24:1-27	The disciples do the tourist thing about the temple, and he says none of it will be left standing. Up on the Mount of Olives they ask when. First answer: watch out for fakes, lots of people will claim the title. Wars, famines, plagues, earthquakes? Just the early contractions. What's coming for them is arrest, execution, being hated everywhere, people turning each other in, and love going cold, with the message reaching every nation first. When Daniel's abomination is standing in the holy place, get out of Judea and don't go back inside for your stuff, and he says plainly how brutal that will be for pregnant and nursing women. Fake messiahs will have great special effects. Next time on Matthew: what the real arrival looks like.
MAT 24:28-51	You won't need a tip-off. Vultures find the body. Lightning crosses the entire sky. Sun and moon go dark, stars fall, the Son of Man shows up on the clouds, trumpet sounds, angels collect people from all four directions. Fig tree leafs out, you know summer's coming. And no, nobody gets the date, not the angels, only the Father. Think Noah: not the flood, the boring part before it, everybody eating and getting married right up to the water. Two guys in a field, two women at the mill, one of each gone. Stay awake, because burglars don't book appointments. Ends on the manager left in charge: either he kept feeding everyone, or he decided the boss was running late and started throwing his weight around.
MAT 25:1-25	Ten bridesmaids wait on a bridegroom who's running late, and the only thing separating the smart five from the rest is a spare bottle of oil. Everyone falls asleep. Midnight, the shout goes up, and the five without oil head off to buy some. Door shuts while they're gone. Watch, he says, nobody knows the hour. Then the money story: three servants get five talents, two, and one, based on what each can handle. First two go trade and double it, and both get the same "well done." The third clearly rehearsed his speech: you're a hard man, you harvest what you never planted, so I buried your cash in the yard, here it is, untouched. Next time on Matthew: how that goes over.
MAT 25:26-46	It goes badly. If the boss is really the guy you just described, he says, you'd have at least put it in a bank and collected interest. The talent goes to the servant already holding ten, because having gets you more, and the third guy is thrown out. Then the big scene: Son of Man on the throne, every nation in front of him, sorted like a shepherd splitting sheep from goats. And the test isn't religious at all. Food, water, a bed for a stranger, clothes, a hospital visit, a prison visit. Both sides ask the same question, when did we ever see you, and get the same answer from opposite ends: the least of these was me. That's the whole list.
MAT 26:1-39	Two days before Passover, Jesus says flatly what's coming. Meanwhile the chief priests meet at Caiaphas's place and agree to grab him quietly, not during the festival, because of the crowds. At Bethany a woman pours a jar of costly ointment over his head. The disciples do the math about the poor, and Jesus says she's prepared him for burial. Judas names his own price: thirty pieces of silver. At the meal Jesus says one of them will hand him over, then gives them bread as his body and the cup as his blood. He tells them they'll all scatter and tells Peter he has until the rooster. In Gethsemane he asks for the cup to pass. Next time on Matthew: the arrest.
MAT 26:40-75	Three times he prays, three times he comes back and his three closest friends are asleep. Then Judas shows up with an armed crowd and identifies him with a kiss. Somebody draws a sword and takes off the high priest's servant's ear, and Jesus shuts it down: put it away, and no, he isn't calling twelve legions of angels. He asks why they brought clubs for a man who taught in the temple every single day. Then every disciple runs. The trial can't find a usable witness until two produce a line about tearing down the temple. Under oath he answers, and the high priest rips his own clothes. They spit on him and hit him. Out in the courtyard Peter denies him three times, and leaves crying.
MAT 27:1-32	Morning, and the priests hand him to Pilate. Judas tries to return the thirty pieces, says outright he betrayed an innocent man, and gets told that's his problem. He throws the silver into the sanctuary and hangs himself. The priests won't put blood money in the treasury, so they buy a field to bury strangers in. Before Pilate, Jesus answers one question and then goes silent, which unnerves the governor. Pilate offers the crowd Barabbas or Jesus. His wife sends a note about a dream. The priests work the crowd, which picks Barabbas and shouts for crucifixion, and Pilate washes his hands, which fixes nothing. Then the soldiers robe him, crown him with thorns, mock and hit him, and pull Simon of Cyrene out to carry the cross. Next time on Matthew: Golgotha.
MAT 27:33-66	He turns down the wine mixed with gall. Crucified between two robbers, soldiers rolling dice for his clothes, a sign over his head naming the charge. Everybody says the same thing in different words: save yourself, come down, then we'll believe. Darkness sits over the land for three hours. Then he cries out asking why God has abandoned him, and people mistake it for a call for Elijah. One more cry, and he's gone. The temple curtain rips top to bottom, the ground shakes, tombs open. The centurion running the execution is the one who says it: this really was the Son of God. The women who followed from Galilee watch from a distance. Joseph of Arimathaea gives up his own new tomb. Then the priests get the stone sealed and guards posted.
MAT 28:1-20	Two women get to the tomb at dawn and walk into an earthquake, an angel who shoves the stone aside and then sits on it, and guards frozen stiff with fear. The message is four lines: not here, risen, come look, go tell them Galilee. They run off scared and thrilled at the same time, and Jesus meets them on the road himself. Meanwhile the guards go report to the chief priests, who pay cash for a better version: the disciples stole the body while we were asleep. Which means admitting they slept on the job. The eleven head to a mountain in Galilee, worship him, and Matthew says flatly that some of them still doubted. Then the last instructions: all authority, go to every nation, baptize, teach, and I'm with you to the end of the age.
PRO 1:1-33	The book opens with a syllabus: wisdom, discernment, justice, fairness, street smarts for beginners, and how to crack a riddle. Prerequisite: fear of Yahweh. Then dad's first real lesson is about peer pressure and it's shockingly violent. If the guys say come lurk with us, jump an innocent stranger, we'll split the loot one purse, the answer is no, because the trap they're setting is going to close on them. Then Wisdom personally takes a street corner and starts yelling at the city gates: how long are you simple people planning to enjoy being simple? Her closer is cold: I called, nobody showed up, so I'm going to laugh when the storm hits.
PRO 2:1-22	One giant if-then sentence. If you take these words, stash them, yell for discernment and dig for wisdom the way people dig for silver, then fear of Yahweh becomes something you actually get instead of just repeating. After that discretion is basically your bodyguard. Two specific hazards it keeps you clear of: guys who genuinely enjoy being evil and went crooked on purpose, and the smooth talker whose house has a downhill exit and no return traffic. Then it ends on property law: the honest stay in the land, the treacherous get pulled out roots and all.
PRO 3:1-35	The chapter everybody knows half of. Trust Yahweh with your whole heart, stop leaning on your own take, and he straightens the road, which sounds easy right up until you try it. Wear kindness and truth like jewelry and write them on your heart. Discipline gets rebranded as affection, because a dad corrects the kid he loves, a line that hits differently depending on the dad. Wisdom beats silver, gold and rubies, holds long life in one hand and cash in the other, and is the tool the earth got built with. Then, out of nowhere, the practical stuff: pay your neighbour today, don't tell him to come back tomorrow when the money's right there.
PRO 4:1-27	Dad cites his sources, which is classy: this is what MY dad told me, back when I was the sweet only child. The pitch is buy wisdom at literally any price, everything you own if that's the number, because she pays out in garlands and crowns. On bad company he does not hedge: don't walk that road carefully, don't stroll past it, go around, because those guys can't fall asleep until they've tripped somebody. Then the best line in the chapter: the good road brightens like sunrise heading for noon, while the wicked keep stubbing their toes on stuff they can't see. Guard your heart, eyes forward, no swerving left or right.
PRO 5:1-23	The adultery chapter, and it runs on aftertaste. Her lips drip honey, her mouth's smoother than oil, and the customer review is: wormwood, and also a double-edged sword. Don't go near the door, because the bill comes due in your honour, your years and your money, and strangers get to enjoy all of it. It even hands you the deathbed regret in advance: man, I hated instruction, I blew off every teacher who tried. The counteroffer is domestic and, for this book, weirdly sweet: drink from your own well, keep your springs on your own property, enjoy the wife of your youth, described as a loving doe. God's watching all the paths anyway, and your own sins do the tying up.
PRO 6:1-35	Assorted emergencies chapter. Co-signing a stranger's loan is treated like a hostage crisis: don't sleep, don't rest, wiggle out of it like a bird escaping a net. Then the lazy guy gets sent on a field trip to watch an ant, who has zero managers and zero meetings and has already finished the summer stockpile, while your ruin shows up like an armed robber after a little nap and a little hand-folding. Next up, the creep who winks and signals with his feet and stirs up drama, who is going to snap one day with no repair option. Then the numbered list: six things God hates, seven that are disgusting, and the one saved for last place is starting fights between brothers.
PRO 7:1-27	Dad quits the theory and tells a story, from his window, through the lattice, a detail he absolutely did not have to include. Kid with no sense takes the route past a certain corner at dusk. The woman who meets him gets the best speech in the chapter: my offerings are paid up, I came out looking specifically for you, the couch has Egyptian tapestry, the bed's got myrrh and aloes and cinnamon, and my husband is out of town with a bag of money and won't be back till the full moon. Every detail is reassurance and the last one closes the deal. Kid follows like an ox to slaughter. Her house is on a downhill street.
PRO 8:1-36	Wisdom grabs the mic and makes her own case, posted up where the roads meet and at the city gates. Nothing crooked in her mouth, better ROI than silver, gold and rubies, and a straight-up flex that kings rule by her. Then the part theologians never stopped fighting about: she was around before the deeps, before the springs, before the mountains got set down, before there was even dust. When the sea got its boundary line she was the craftsman right beside him, delighted every single day, thrilled with the finished world and specifically with people. Find her, you find life. Everybody else, she says, is in love with death.
PRO 9:1-18	Two dinner invites, printed in the same font on purpose. Wisdom builds a house, carves seven pillars, cooks the meat, mixes the wine, sets the table and sends staff out to yell for the clueless. Folly sits in her own doorway in the same high spots of town and uses literally the same words, and her only menu item is that stolen water tastes sweet and secret snacks are great. The difference isn't the pitch, it's the guest list, because hers are already down in Sheol. Sandwiched between the two invites is the driest advice in the book: correct a mocker, get insulted. Correct a wise guy, he likes you for it.
PRO 10:1-32	The one-liners kick off, and the format is hard binary: righteous on one side, wicked on the other, no middle. Main obsessions are work and mouths. Lazy hand makes you broke, busy hand makes you rich, and sleeping through harvest is framed as a personality defect, not a scheduling issue. On talking it's basically modern: pile up enough words and you'll never run short of sin, shutting up is wisdom, hidden hate makes you a liar, and love covers what hate keeps poking at. God's blessing is described as money with no strings attached, a distinction anyone who's come into cash will appreciate. Best line of the chapter: a lazy employee is vinegar on the teeth and smoke in the eyes of whoever sent him.
PRO 11:1-31	Opens with rigged scales, because nothing exposes your actual theology faster than a thumb on the weights. Then it goes civic: the city throws a party when good people do well and gets wrecked by the mouths of the bad ones, and a nation with no advisors just falls over. Gossip gets an actual definition, betraying a confidence, which beats vaguely calling it bad. The generosity math shouldn't work but does: scatter and you get more, water people and you get watered, hoard the grain and the whole neighbourhood curses you by name. Money, it says twice, is worthless on judgment day. And then the line nobody ever forgets: a gold ring in a pig's nose.
PRO 12:1-28	The collection hits its stride and starts name-calling, because hating correction isn't "unfortunate" here, it's stupid. Domestic notes: good wife is a crown, bad wife is rot in the bones, which is lopsided but you'll remember it. There's a quietly lovely line about a righteous man taking care of his animal. On work: chasing fantasies versus actually farming dirt, and the lazy hunter gets caught not for failing to hunt but for not bothering to cook what he shot. On temper, the fool shows his annoyance the same day, which is the most modern thing in the chapter. Rash words stab like a sword, kind words lift a heavy heart.
PRO 13:1-25	Mouths, money, and the rod. Guard your mouth, guard your life. Fling your lips open, you're done. Sharp pair about faking it: one guy performing rich on nothing, another performing broke on a fortune, which reads like something someone actually watched happen. Dirty money shrinks, hand-gathered money grows, an ancient argument about compounding. The famous one shows up here: hope deferred makes the heart sick, and getting what you longed for is a tree of life, easily the most emotionally accurate sentence in the book so far. Also: hang out with wise people and you get wise, which is either encouraging or brutal depending on your contacts list.
PRO 14:1-35	This chapter keeps admitting the system isn't tidy. Your heart knows its own bitterness and won't hand it to a stranger. Laughter can be covering something. There's a road that looks totally correct and ends in death, which quietly undercuts every confident proverb around it. Simple people believe everything, careful people stop and check. Best farm line: no oxen means a clean stall, and also no harvest, which is a whole essay on the cost of anything worth having. It's unsentimental about cash too, noting broke people lose even their neighbour while rich people collect friends, then turns around and says squeezing the poor is contempt for their Maker.
PRO 15:1-33	A whole chapter about tone of voice, which sounds impossible to stretch to thirty-three verses and somehow works. The gentle answer defusing rage kicks it off, and the theme keeps circling back: righteous people weigh their answers, wicked people just gush, and a well-timed word is described as a pleasure, not a chore. God's eyes are everywhere, and since Sheol and Abaddon are already open for inspection, your heart is not exactly a challenge. Then the two comparisons that carry this whole book's economics: better a little with the fear of the Lord than a big pile with stress attached, and better a plate of herbs where there's love than steak served with hate.
PRO 16:1-33	This one's about the gap between planning and happening. You pick the route, God moves your feet. Everybody's ways look spotless to the guy walking them, which is exactly why somebody else weighs the motives. There's a block of court survival tips for anyone working near a king: his anger is a death sentence, his favour is a rain cloud, so read the face carefully. Then the greatest hits land basically back to back: pride before destruction, arrogance before the fall, nice words as a honeycomb, grey hair as a crown you can only earn by living a certain way. Best of the bunch is the quietest one: controlling your own temper beats conquering a city.
PRO 17:1-28	Opens with the single most convincing argument in the book: a dry crust in peace beats a banquet with a fight going on. Great concrete conflict rule too, starting a quarrel is breaching a dam, so shut it before the water's moving. Two images do the heavy lifting: a rebuke sinks deeper into a wise guy than a hundred lashes into a fool, and it's safer to run into a bear whose cubs got stolen than a fool mid-meltdown. Mocking poor people gets filed as insulting their Maker. A friend loves you all the time. And the last verse is weirdly generous to fools: keep your mouth shut and people will just assume you're smart.
PRO 18:1-24	Mostly a chapter about talking, and it's shockingly sharp about how arguments actually go. A fool gets zero pleasure from understanding and all his pleasure from sharing his opinion, a sentence that has aged extremely well. Answering before you've listened gets called shameful. And there's real procedural insight: whoever talks first sounds totally right, until somebody cross-examines him. Gossip goes down like fancy little snacks, which is exactly why it works. The rich guy's wall is unscalable in his own imagination, six words doing serious damage. An offended brother is harder to get into than a fortified city. And one friend can stick tighter than a brother.
PRO 19:1-29	Poverty is the running theme and the treatment is bleak. Money collects friends. Broke guys lose even their relatives, and chase them with pleas that reach nobody. The counterweight is a single line: pity the poor and you've made a loan to God, who settles up. Elsewhere, zeal without knowledge gets its own warning, which basically no sermon ever quotes. The bit about the hot-tempered guy is uncomfortably practical: bail him out of the consequences and congratulations, you've signed up to do it again. There's a continual dripping where a quarrelsome wife is concerned. And then the sluggard, who's got his hand in the dish and can't see the point of the trip back to his mouth.
PRO 20:1-30	Wine's a mocker and beer's a brawler, which is the entire hangover in six words. Honest weights come up twice, in case you missed it. The genuinely interesting thread is how little you know yourself: who can claim a clean heart, how can you understand your own path when your steps come from somewhere else, and the human spirit described as God's flashlight going through your drawers. One bit of straight comedy: the buyer going "nah, it's junk, it's junk" and then bragging about the deal the second he's around the corner. Don't pay back evil, it says. Wait. And grey hair is the glory of old men.
PRO 21:1-31	Opens by describing a king's heart as plumbing, a water channel God redirects wherever he wants, which is comforting for everyone except the king. Doing justice gets rated above sacrifice, which is a bolder move than it looks in a book this pious. Lot of housing advice too: the corner of the roof beats sharing a house with a contentious woman, and failing that, literal desert, a line the editor liked enough to run twice. The sluggard gets killed by his own cravings, his hands having declined to participate. And the last verse is the whole book compressed: prep the horse anyway, but the result isn't yours.
PRO 22:1-29	A good reputation beats money, and rich and poor came off the same assembly line, which is the kind of levelling this book does without making a fuss about it. There's the train-up-a-child verse, and the cold economic take that the borrower is the lender's servant, which needs no theology at all. The sluggard returns with the greatest excuse in scripture: there's a lion. Outside. In the street. Then the format shifts, sentences get longer, and the collection announces itself as thirty sayings. Best one has teeth: shaking down the poor in court gets answered not with a rule but with a warning that God will take the case personally. Also don't befriend an angry guy, in case it's contagious.
PRO 23:1-35	Dinner etiquette for the ambitious, and it's grim. Watch what gets set in front of you. If your host is cheap, the food he's pushing is food you'll be throwing up later, along with all your compliments. Don't wear yourself out getting rich, because money grows eagle wings and leaves. Then the boundary stone, backed by the note that orphans have a very good lawyer. And finally the wine passage, the best writing about drinking anywhere: who's got misery, arguments, bruises he can't explain, bloodshot eyes? Don't look at it when it sparkles and goes down easy, because at the end it bites like a snake. Ends on the guy who can't feel the beating he took and is already lining up the next round.
PRO 24:1-34	House built by wisdom, rooms furnished by knowledge, wars won by having enough advisors, all fair. Then the chapter does something this book mostly dodges and demands you actually do something: go rescue the people being marched off to die, and don't try the "well, we didn't know" line, because the guy who weighs hearts has already thought about that. Elsewhere it's generous. A righteous man falls seven times and gets back up, which is permission, not a performance target. Don't enjoy your enemy's crash, on the grounds that God might notice and lose interest. And the closing walk past the sluggard's vineyard, thorns, nettles, wall caved in, is a whole sermon delivered as scenery.
PRO 25:1-28	Second collection, credited to Hezekiah's copy department, and the imagery gets a serious upgrade. Starts at court with genuinely useful advice: don't seat yourself high, because getting invited up beats getting moved down while the prince watches. Then the metaphors come in a burst. A well-timed word is gold apples in silver settings. A reliable messenger is snow in harvest. A guy who brags about gifts is cloud and wind with no rain. Singing cheerful songs at someone grieving is like stealing their coat in the cold. Feed your hungry enemy and pile coals on his head, which is either kindness or the best revenge on offer. A guy with no self-control is a city with the walls knocked down.
PRO 26:1-28	Twenty-eight verses on fools and the editor has stopped being nice. Honour on a fool sits like snow in July. Two verses back to back give completely opposite instructions about answering him, which is either a scribe's joke or an honest admission that both options stink. Best images: sending a message via a fool is cutting off your own feet, a proverb in his mouth flops like a lame man's legs, and a fool going back to his folly is a dog returning to its vomit, which nobody has ever improved on. Meanwhile the sluggard has upgraded his lion and now flips on his bed like a door on hinges. Butting into a fight that isn't yours is grabbing a strange dog by the ears.
PRO 27:1-27	A chapter of unusually great sentences. Don't brag about tomorrow. Let somebody else do the praising. A fool's provocation outweighs stone and sand, and jealousy outweighs everything. Open rebuke beats hidden love, and a friend's wounds get called faithful, the nicest thing this book ever says about criticism. Completely practical note: a nearby neighbour beats a faraway brother on the day it all goes wrong. Also a small comedy gem, bless a guy loudly first thing in the morning and he'll take it as a curse. Iron sharpens iron. Sheol, Abaddon and human eyeballs are the three things never satisfied, quite the group photo. And grind a fool in a mortar with a pestle and congratulations, you have fool paste.
PRO 28:1-28	Opens with the wicked sprinting away from absolutely nobody, the most psychologically sharp image in the whole collection. After that it's mostly money and how it warps whoever's holding it. Rich guy thinks he's brilliant; the broke guy with sense sees right through him. Loan-shark interest gets quietly reassigned to somebody who'll actually pity the poor. There's a rare tender line: hiding your sins doesn't work, owning them and dropping them gets mercy. And two political shots that haven't aged a day: a bad ruler over defenceless people is a roaring lion and a charging bear, and people go into hiding every time the wicked move up in the world.
PRO 29:1-27	Basically a chapter about government. The people cheer or groan depending entirely on who's in charge. A king who judges the poor fairly keeps his throne, one who takes bribes dismantles the country, and a ruler who'll listen to lies ends up with a staff of exclusively liars, presented as cause and effect, not bad luck. Elsewhere it gets personal and sharp: flattery is a net laid for your feet, a fool dumps out all his anger while a wise guy holds it, and taking a fool to court gets you rage and sneering but no verdict. The no-vision-people-run-wild line is famous. The one about hasty talk being worse than being a fool deserves to be.
PRO 30:1-33	Agur son of Jakeh shows up and he's the most interesting voice in the book. Opens by announcing he's the dumbest guy in the room and asking who exactly has gathered up the wind in his fists. His prayer is one of the few genuinely modest asks in scripture: no lies, and no poverty and no riches either, because full people deny God and hungry people steal. Then he goes hard on lists, which he clearly loves. Four things that never say enough, ending with fire. Four things too amazing to figure out, ending with a man and a young woman. Four tiny creatures that are wise anyway, including a lizard who somehow got into a palace. Closing note: forcing anger makes strife like twisting a nose makes blood.
PRO 31:1-31	King Lemuel's mom gets the last word and she doesn't hedge. Not women, not wine, because a drinking king forgets the law and the person who eats that loss is whoever's already suffering. Save the hard liquor for people who are actually perishing. Then: speak up for the people who have no voice. The closing poem about the worthy woman gets read as domestic and is honestly mostly commercial. She scouts a field and buys it. Plants a vineyard. Runs a linen operation with the merchants. Works past dark. Imports food like a shipping company. Her husband's contribution is being well regarded at the gate. Charm is fake, beauty's vapour, and the ask at the end is just: pay her and credit her in public.
SIR 1:1-30	Sirach opens the way a scribe opens a career, by making clear the material isn't his: all wisdom comes from the Lord and stays with him. Unanswerable questions first, because nobody's counting the sand or the raindrops or the days of eternity. Wisdom, we learn, predates literally everything, got created and then counted and poured out over all God's works, which is a weirdly industrial image and a good one. Then the phrase this book will never let go of, the fear of the Lord, which inside twenty verses gets called wisdom's beginning, its fullness, its crown and its root, apparently to leave no gardening metaphor unused. Ends on practicalities: unfair anger is never justifiable, and self-promotion gets your secrets read out in public.
SIR 2:1-18	Advice for anybody who just signed up to serve God, and item one is that it's going to be rough: get your soul ready for temptation. Gold gets tested in fire and acceptable men in the furnace of humiliation, which is honest and zero comfort. Then he addresses the God-fearing three times in a row, wait, trust, hope, and backs it with a challenge: name one person from history who trusted and got humiliated for it. The woes that follow target hesitation, not wickedness: scared hearts, limp hands, the sinner walking two roads at once, and anybody who's run out of patience. And the closing preference is a real one: better to land in God's hands than in people's hands.
SIR 3:1-31	A chapter on parents that's unusually honest about what honour actually costs. The famous part is about a father's decline: help him, don't grieve him, and if his mind starts going, be patient instead of humiliating him while you're still strong. That's the kind of instruction that only comes from somebody who watched it happen. The reward gets described beautifully, sins melting like ice in good weather. Then a pivot to humility with a line that has aged extremely well, the bigger you are the more you humble yourself, followed by advice against chasing things too hard for you, which coming from a wisdom teacher is a strikingly modest take.
SIR 4:1-31	First half is about poverty and it does not hedge. Don't make hungry eyes wait. Don't pile trouble onto a heart that's already had some. Don't turn your face away, and don't give anybody a reason to curse you, because whoever made him is listening. Be a dad to the fatherless and stand in for the husband, and you'll get treated like a son of the Most High. Then wisdom shows up as an instructor with alarming methods, walking her student through crooked routes first, bringing fear and dread and grinding him with discipline until she decides he's trustworthy, and only then coming back by the straight road with the secrets. Also a great distinction between shame that's sin and shame that's grace.
SIR 5:1-15	A list of sentences not to say out loud, which makes this one of the more fun chapters here. Don't say your stuff is enough. Don't ask who's in charge of you. And definitely don't say the thing everyone's thought, that you sinned and nothing happened, because the right reading of that silence is patience, not permission. Don't say his compassion is huge and will cover the whole pile, because mercy and wrath share an office. The imagery for being indecisive is agricultural and exact: don't winnow with every wind, don't walk down every path. And if you don't understand something, the instruction is literally to put your hand over your mouth.
SIR 6:1-37	The friendship chapter, and it's the most clear-eyed thing anybody wrote on the subject in the ancient world. Keep lots of people on good terms with you, and one actual advisor in a thousand. Get a friend by testing him first, because the taxonomy is brutal: the friend who's in it for his own convenience, the friend who converts to enemy and takes the fight public, and the dinner buddy who mirrors your success, starts bossing your staff around, and then can't be located the second things go bad. Against that, a faithful friend is a medicine of life. Second half switches to wisdom and does not soften her, because she's harsh to the unlearned and lands like a testing stone, and the instruction is to stick your own neck in her chain.
SIR 7:1-36	A very long list of don'ts, in the voice of a guy who has watched people wreck themselves in every single one of these ways. Don't angle for a promotion. Don't show off your wisdom in front of the king. Don't volunteer to be a judge in case you turn out to be scared of a powerful face. Don't assume a big enough offering gets waved through on volume. Don't repeat your words in prayer, which is a shrewd note. The household section is brisker and less comfortable, since daughters get watched and sons get their necks bowed from youth, but it also insists on paying a hired man properly. And the closing line is the book's whole method: remember your last end.
SIR 8:1-19	A chapter entirely about picking your fights, and its conclusion is to pick basically none. Not the powerful guy, not the rich guy, not the guy who won't stop talking, because piling wood on that fire is exactly what he was after. Don't sue a judge, on the grounds that the verdict will follow his honour rather than the evidence, a cynical and probably accurate read on the courts. Don't lend upward, and if you have to, treat the money as gone. Don't cross a desert with an angry man, because blood means nothing to him and there'll be nobody around to help. The kindest lines are about aging and dying: don't mock the old or gloat over the dead, because we're all on the list.
SIR 9:1-18	The chapter every modern reader trips over, because the advice about women is framed relentlessly as hazard management: don't stare, don't linger, don't drink with a married woman, don't hang around with a singer. At least the reasoning is stated honestly, that beauty has led plenty astray and love catches like fire, which puts the problem in the looker. Then it pivots, and the second half is genuinely warm. Never swap an old friend for a new one, because a new friend is new wine and only worth drinking once it's aged. Don't envy a sinner's win, because you have no idea what his collapse looks like. And stay well clear of anybody with the power to kill you.
SIR 10:1-31	Political theory, and the core claim is that a people takes its character from whoever's running it: as the judge is, so are his officials, and as the ruler is, so is the entire city. An uninstructed king wrecks his people. Then a long and enjoyable demolition of pride, tracking empires changing hands over injustice, violence and greed. Best pair of lines in the chapter: he's a king today, tomorrow he's dead, and what he inherits is creeping things and worms. God, it says, has been yanking thrones out from under rulers and installing the meek for a very long time. Pride was never issued to humans in the first place, and honour attaches to fear of the Lord, rich or poor.
SIR 11:1-34	A chapter about not judging the surface, and it argues the case with a bee, which is tiny among flying things and still makes the best thing on the table. Kings have wound up sitting on the dirt and total nobodies have worn crowns. There's excellent procedural advice in here: check the facts before you blame, listen before you answer, and don't interrupt. Then the parable of the saver, who scrimps for years, announces he's finally going to enjoy his stuff, and turns out to have no idea how little time is left. Don't call anybody blessed before they're dead. And the best image in the chapter: the proud man as a decoy partridge in a cage, sitting there waiting to be the reason somebody else gets caught.
SIR 12:1-18	Two subjects, generosity and enemies, and the first one is uncomfortable. Know who you're giving to, help the godly and the lowly, and specifically withhold bread from the ungodly, on the practical grounds that he'll use it against you. Then the enemy section, a small masterpiece of paranoia. His wickedness is rust on brass, and even if he crouches and acts humble, you'll find when you wipe the mirror that the rust never actually came off. Don't seat him next to you and definitely not on your right. He talks sweetly while scouting you for a pit, he cries actual tears, and when trouble shows up he's already standing there, offering a hand and putting a foot behind your heel.
SIR 13:1-26	The class chapter, and it's merciless. Touch pitch, you're marked. Hang around the rich and powerful and you're the clay pot next to the iron kettle, and only one of you survives that collision. The portrait of the patron is the best thing here and reads like reporting: he trades on you while you're useful, flatters you when he needs something, humiliates you with his own hospitality until he's stripped you two or three times, then laughs, and later shakes his head at you in the street. When a rich guy falls there's a whole line of helpers and his indiscretions get explained away. When a poor guy falls he gets a lecture, and his wisdom gets no room at all.
SIR 14:1-27	The miser's portrait, and it's a study in self-harm more than greed. His eye is never satisfied with his own share, he grudges the bread on his own table, and everything he saves by starving himself is money somebody else will eventually enjoy. There's nobody worse off, the author says, than the guy who envies himself. The counter-advice is unexpectedly generous for this book: be good to yourself within your means, be generous to your friend while you're still breathing, and don't waste a good day, because nobody goes hunting for luxury in the grave. Generations turn over like leaves on a thick tree. And wisdom, in the closing image, gets straight up stalked: windows, doorways, a tent pitched next door.
SIR 15:1-20	Wisdom gets described in domestic terms, meeting the law-keeping man like a mother and receiving him like a bride, feeding him bread of understanding and water of wisdom, and opening his mouth in the assembly, which for a scribe is the actual prize. Then the chapter gets to the argument it exists for. Don't say God made you fall. Don't say he made you mess up, because he has no use for a sinful man. God made humans and left them in the hand of their own counsel, one of the strongest free-will statements in the whole tradition. Fire and water are laid out on the table; grab whichever one you want. Nobody was commanded to be ungodly and nobody got issued a permit.
SIR 16:1-30	Opens bleak about family: better to die childless than raise ungodly kids, and one good one beats a thousand of the other kind. Then the historical evidence, delivered like a court docket: the giants who revolted in their strength, the neighbours Lot had to live next to, and the six hundred thousand footmen who managed it purely through hardness of heart. The interesting part is the objection he sees coming, because somebody's going to say there's no way God notices one guy in a creation this size, and the answer is that heaven, the deep and the earth all move when he shows up. Chapter ends on creation as a workforce that never gets hungry, never gets tired, never elbows its coworkers, and has never once failed to do what it was told.
SIR 17:1-32	A compressed anthropology. Man is made of dirt and goes back to dirt, with days issued by the number, authority over everything living here, and a kit list of equipment: counsel, tongue, eyes, ears, heart. He got shown good and evil, handed a law of life as an inheritance and an everlasting covenant, and is under continuous observation, since all his works are like the sun in front of God. Best image is administrative and honestly lovely: your charitable giving gets kept like a signet ring and guarded like the pupil of an eye. And the case for repenting now is entirely practical, not pious: dead people don't say thanks, and only somebody alive and healthy is in a position to praise anyone.
SIR 18:1-33	Opens with a great statement of the limits of the whole wisdom project: when a man finishes, he's only at the beginning, and when he stops he'll be confused. A hundred years max, one drop from the sea, one pebble off the beach, and God's patience gets presented as a direct result of noticing how short we are. Then the practical section, which has one of the best lines in the book: a word is better than a gift. Plus the observation that an envious man's present consumes your eyes. Then timing advice: learn before you talk, humble yourself before you get sick, pay the vow now, remember hunger while you're full. Also, don't throw banquets on credit.
SIR 19:1-30	Opens with a hard economic fact, a drunk worker does not get rich, plus a note that despising small stuff ruins you gradually instead of all at once. Then the discretion chapter proper, and it's very good. Never repeat what you were told, friend or enemy, because he heard you and watched you and will get around to hating you at a convenient moment. A secret can safely die with you. It won't make you explode. The fool, though, labours with a secret like a woman in childbirth, and it sits in him like an arrow in the thigh. There's also generous advice about correcting people: reprove before you threaten, because maybe he didn't say it, and everyone has sinned with their mouth.
SIR 20:1-32	A chapter built almost entirely on silence, and it sorts out the different kinds. One guy is quiet because he's got nothing. Another is quiet because he knows his moment hasn't come. The braggart talks straight through his. Then a run of paradoxes: prosperity found inside a disaster, profit that becomes a loss, a gift that pays back nothing and a gift that pays back double, a bargain that ends up costing sevenfold. The fool's generosity gets a full portrait: gives little, scolds a lot, announces it like a town crier, asks for it back the next day, then complains he has no friends. Best line: slipping on the pavement beats slipping with your tongue. And better to hide your folly than hide your wisdom.
SIR 21:1-28	Sin gets the snake treatment, with lion teeth attached, and the point is proximity: it only bites if you get close. There's a genuinely great line about a poor man's prayer landing in God's ears with the verdict arriving right behind it. The economics is sharp too: building your house with other people's money is compared to stockpiling rocks for winter. But most of the chapter is a portrait gallery of the fool, and it's unusually vivid. His insides are a cracked pot that won't hold knowledge. Instruction sits on him like handcuffs and on a wise guy like gold jewelry. He laughs at full volume where a smart man barely smiles. And his foot is inside somebody else's house before anyone invited him. Best closer: the heart of fools is in their mouth.
SIR 22:1-27	Opens by comparing a lazy man first to a filthy stone and then to something off a dunghill, which suggests the author had a specific guy in mind. Teaching a fool gets described as gluing a broken pot back together, or waking a guy out of deep sleep who then goes, huh, what? Then the line everybody quotes: mourn the dead seven days and a fool his entire life, because at least the dead are resting. Sand, salt and a block of iron are easier to carry. Second half switches to friendship and is oddly forgiving, because even a drawn sword can be walked back, though arrogance, spilling a secret and a treacherous shot cannot. He signs off asking for a guard on his own mouth.
SIR 23:1-28	A prayer opens it, and what he asks not to get is very specific: a proud look, greed, a shameless mind. Then the discipline of the mouth, and the target is casual swearing, on the grounds that a guy who's constantly naming God is like a servant who's constantly getting beaten, always carrying a bruise. The rest is about adultery, and the best bit is the internal monologue he writes for the adulterer: walls are up, it's dark, who's watching, what's to be afraid of. The answer is one of the strongest lines in the book: God's eyes are ten thousand times brighter than the sun and they look into secret places. The parallel case of an unfaithful wife gets handled far more harshly, including consequences aimed at her children.
SIR 24:1-34	The centrepiece of the book, and Wisdom delivers it herself. She came out of the mouth of the Most High, covered the earth like mist, kept her throne in the pillar of cloud, walked the circuit of heaven and the floor of the abyss, and then went looking for an address. The Creator assigned her one: Jacob, Israel, Zion. The self-description that follows is all trees and spices, cedar, cypress, palm, Jericho roses, cinnamon, myrrh, frankincense, and it's the loveliest writing in Sirach. Then the paradox: eat her and you'll be hungry again, drink her and you'll be thirsty again. And then the identification the whole book rests on, because all of this turns out to be the law of Moses.
SIR 25:1-26	Starts warm, with three beautiful things: relatives who get along, neighbours who are actually friends, and a couple who genuinely agree. Then three men he can't stand: the arrogant poor guy, the lying rich guy, and the old adulterer with no sense. There's real praise for old age, plus a happiness list of nine items and a tenth, topped by the fear of the Lord. And then the second half shows up, and it's the most misogynistic stretch in the book: he'd rather share a house with a lion than a wicked wife, all malice is nothing next to a woman's, sin and death both started with a woman, and she gets no freedom of speech and should be cut off. There's no joke in that, and no reason to hunt for one.
SIR 26:1-29	The chapter opens generously, with a good wife doubling her husband's lifespan, and produces two of the loveliest images in the book: her beauty running a house like sunrise, and a face in ripe age like the lamp on the holy candlestick. In between sits the author's own list of terrors, and it's worth noticing that it's entirely social: getting slandered around town, facing a hostile crowd, a false accusation, each rated worse than death. There's also another stretch of hostile material about jealous and headstrong women, cut from the same cloth as the last chapter. It ends on three things that make him angry, and the first two genuinely land: a soldier living in poverty, and men of understanding treated like garbage.
SIR 27:1-30	Commercial observation first, and it's memorable: sin wedges itself in between buying and selling the way a nail goes between two stones. Then the assay imagery Sirach can't get enough of, sieve and furnace, applied to conversation, so you don't praise anybody until you've heard them argue. The heart of the chapter is betrayed confidences and the tone is unusually hurt. Break a secret and your friend is already a bird out of your hand, a gazelle already through the snare, and chasing him is pointless. Wounds get bandaged, insults get patched up, this doesn't. The closing physics is the standard kind, rocks thrown up landing on the thrower's head, pits eating their diggers, and it still satisfies.
SIR 28:1-26	An argument about grudges, framed as a question you can't answer: you're feeding a grudge against a guy made of the exact same flesh, and you were planning to ask for mercy yourself? Remember your last end and drop it. The fire imagery is precise: strife burns in proportion to its fuel and in proportion to the wealth of whoever's funding it, and a spark can be blown up or spat out, and both options come out of the same mouth. Then the tongue, and this is the best passage on it anywhere: it's scattered nations, knocked down fortified cities, driven women out of their homes, and where a whip marks skin, a tongue breaks bones. More have fallen by it than by the sword. So install a door and a bar.
SIR 29:1-28	On lending, and the portrait of the borrower is drawn from life: he's kissing your hands until the money's in them, then the due date hits and suddenly the times are hard, and if you ever see half of it back you should count yourself lucky and expect a free enemy in the deal. Which is why, the author notes, people have quit lending entirely. He doesn't accept that as an excuse. Help the poor guy because it's the commandment, and treat what you gave away as treasure in storage that fights better than a shield and a spear. Then the dignity section, the best part: bread, water, clothes and a roof are the essentials, and your own shelter of logs beats great food in a house where you don't dare open your mouth.
SIR 30:1-25	The parenting half has not aged well and there's no point pretending otherwise, because the recommended tools are stripes, a bowed neck and zero freedom, on the theory that an unbroken horse turns stubborn. The stated goal is at least affectionate: a well-taught son continues his father, so the father dies and somehow hasn't died. Then the chapter switches subject entirely and gets genuinely wise. Health beats all the gold there is. A poor man with a solid constitution is better off than a rich invalid. Death beats a bitter life. And his closing advice would pass in a modern clinic: love your own soul, comfort your heart, put the sorrow down, because worry ages you early.
SIR 31:1-31	Money first, and the observation is medical: worrying about your riches eats your flesh and kills your sleep. His test for a rich man is fair and very hard to pass, because it isn't whether he has money, it's whether he had the chance to cheat and passed. Then the etiquette section, the most fun thing in Sirach. Don't reach wherever your eyes go. Don't shove into the dish. Gauge your neighbour's appetite by your own. Stop eating first, for manners. Eating moderately means sleeping well and waking up sharp, while the guy who can't stop gets stomach cramps. On wine he's admirably balanced: as good as life in measure, and what kind of life is it without any, followed by a flat note on what happens if you overdo it.
SIR 32:1-24	A guide to behaving at a party, and it's charming. Got put in charge of the feast? Don't get grand about it, take care of the guests, sit down last. Elders can talk, but with actual knowledge, and nobody talks over the music, which gets two of the book's better similes, a carbuncle in gold and an emerald in gold. The advice to the young guy is the best part: talk only if you're needed, hesitate even when asked twice, pack a lot into a few words, and be the person who knows things and keeps quiet. Then a brisk close: get up early, go home, don't loiter, don't do anything without advice, and don't sit around regretting what's already done.
SIR 33:1-31	A chapter about distinction. Why is one day better than another when all the daylight comes off the same sun? Because God separated them, hallowing some and leaving others regular, and the author runs the same logic on people, who are clay in the potter's hand. The design principle is stated cleanly: the works of the Most High come in pairs, each one against its opposite. The fool gets a great image, a heart like a cartwheel and thoughts like a rolling axle. Then some hard-nosed retirement advice, basically don't sign anything over early, because it's better that your kids ask you than that you have to ask them. On servants he's harsh and then, out of nowhere, insists you treat one like yourself.
SIR 34:1-26	The dream chapter, and the debunking is quick: fixating on a dream is grabbing at a shadow and chasing wind, and fortune-telling is worth nothing unless it came from somewhere else entirely. What he offers instead is experience, and he cites his own, including several occasions of nearly getting killed while travelling, which does more for his credibility than any argument would. The final third turns severe and it's the best part. A sacrifice bought with stolen money is a joke. An offering taken out of poor people's goods is like killing a son in front of his father. Stiffing a labourer on his wages is shedding blood. And fasting for a sin you fully plan to repeat is washing after touching a corpse and then touching it again.
SIR 35:1-20	The argument is that regular behaviour counts as liturgy: keeping the law multiplies offerings, returning a favour is fine flour, almsgiving is a thank offering, and just stopping the wrongdoing works as atonement. He still wants the actual offering, given cheerfully, on the promise of sevenfold repayment. Then the part that matters. Don't imagine the gift buys anything, because the judge takes no bribes and will not side with anybody against a poor man. The widow's tears get described as a cry against whoever caused them, which is a legal formulation, not a sentimental one. And the prayer of the humble punches through the clouds and refuses to leave until the sceptres of the unrighteous get snapped.
SIR 36:1-26	The chapter opens in a completely different register, as a national prayer: send your fear on the nations, show new signs, crush the heads of the rulers who go around saying there's nobody but us, gather the tribes, have compassion on Jerusalem. It's the closest Sirach ever gets to a psalm. Then, with no transition whatsoever, we're back to household advice. The belly will eat anything, but one meat is better than another, and an understanding heart tastes fake talk the way a mouth tastes game, which is the sharpest line here. Marriage gets described as property and shelter, a pillar of rest and a fence around a plot, and the guy without one is a robber skipping town to town, trusted by nobody.
SIR 37:1-31	On advice, and it's the most cynical and useful thing in the book. Everyone claims to be your friend. Some are friends in name only, one enjoys your good times and vanishes in the bad, another works alongside you strictly for the meals. Then the advisors, and the warning is precise: every advisor recommends getting advice, so figure out his angle first, because he may advise in his own favour and then stand next to you watching how it goes. The list of people never to consult is the best bit: a coward about war, a merchant about exchange rates, a lazy guy about work.
