PSA 148:1-14	Universe-wide group chat, top down. Angels, sun, moon, stars, the water above the sky. Then the earth crew: sea monsters, lightning, hail, snow, wind, mountains, fruit trees, cedars, cattle, little creatures, birds. Kings and judges get added way down the list, wedged between the livestock and the teenagers. Same reason for everyone: he said the word and you existed. His name is the only one actually exalted.
PSA 149:1-9	New song, dancing, tambourine, harp, saints singing for joy in bed. Very cozy. Then verse six hands everybody a two-edged sword and the mood shifts fast: vengeance on the nations, kings in chains, nobles in iron shackles, the sentence carried out exactly as written. The psalm calls this an honour that all the saints get. No, it does not walk any of that back.
PSA 150:1-6	The final psalm basically just lists the band. Trumpet, harp, lyre, tambourine, dancing, strings, flute, cymbals, and then louder cymbals, because apparently the first cymbals were too subtle. No complaining, no enemies, nothing to argue about. Thirteen different ways to say praise him in six verses, then the closer: if you have breath, congratulations, you are in the choir.
HOS 1:1-11	God's opening instruction to Hosea is: go marry a prostitute, because the whole country is cheating on me. Hosea marries Gomer, and then names his three kids after the indictment. Jezreel, for the blood about to be avenged. Lo-Ruhamah, which is basically No Mercy. Lo-Ammi, which is Not My People. Imagine that at the school gate. Then the chapter pivots hard and promises Israel will be uncountable as sand and called sons of the living God after all.
HOS 2:1-23	Now the marriage thing gets used as a weapon. Israel is the wife who ran off after lovers for bread, wool and wine, without ever noticing her actual husband was the one stocking the pantry. Punishment list: stripped bare, festivals cancelled, vineyards turned into scrubland. Then the mood flips completely. Wilderness date, sweet talk, the valley of Achor rebranded as a door of hope, and a permanent engagement. Not my people gets officially cancelled and replaced with: you are my people.
HOS 3:1-5	Do it again, says God. Go love the adulteress. So Hosea buys her, for fifteen pieces of silver and a homer and a half of barley, which is grocery money. Terms: you're with me and nobody else, and same goes for me. The point being made is that Israel keeps chasing other gods and raisin cakes, and is going to spend a long stretch with no king, no sacrifice and no idols before it comes crawling back.
HOS 4:1-19	God files charges against the whole country. Exhibit A: nothing true, nothing good, nobody who knows him. Exhibit B: cursing, lying, murder, theft, adultery, and blood on top of blood, until even the fish are dying. The priests get the worst of it, because they're literally eating off everyone's sins, and the verdict is like people, like priest. Israel is a stubborn cow. Ephraim is so welded to its idols that God gives up and says just leave him alone.
HOS 5:1-15	Priests, people, palace, all three hauled in front of the bench. Brutal detail: they can't turn back because their own habits won't let them. They show up with flocks and herds to find God and he's not home. He then describes himself as a moth, then as rot, then as a lion that shreds you and walks off, which is quite the ramp-up. Ephraim's big idea is to ask Assyria for help. Assyria cannot help.
1CH 13:1-14	David polls every captain first, because this Chronicler's David never does anything alone. Then the ark goes on a brand new cart, which is the one detail nobody checked. Oxen stumble, Uzza grabs to steady it, and Uzza drops dead on the spot. David is furious, then scared, and parks the ark at Obed-Edom's house instead. Three months later Obed-Edom's household is thriving, which is the hint David eventually takes.
1CH 14:1-17	Hiram of Tyre ships cedar and workmen, and David reads that as proof the job is real. More wives, more sons, and the Jerusalem list includes Solomon without comment. Philistines come hunting him twice. First time God says go, and David names the place Broke Out Like Water. Second time God says no, circle round behind the mulberry trees, and wait for the sound of marching in the treetops before you move.
1CH 15:1-29	Take two on the ark, and this time David reads the manual. Levites only, carried on shoulders with poles like Moses said, because last time they skipped the rules and it cost a man his life. Then a full band: Heman, Asaph and Ethan on cymbals, harps tuned eight-string, Chenaniah running the choir because he was good at it. Seven bulls and seven rams. Michal watches David dancing and privately writes him off.
1CH 16:1-43	Ark parked, offerings burned, and everyone in Israel goes home with bread, meat and a raisin cake. Then David hands Asaph a thanksgiving psalm stitched from bits you have read before: give thanks, remember the covenant, all the other gods are idols, let the trees of the forest sing. The people say amen. Asaph stays with the ark, Zadok stays at Gibeon with the altar, and David goes home to bless his own house.
1CH 17:1-27	David notices he lives in cedar while the ark lives in canvas. Nathan says go for it, then gets corrected that same night. God's answer is a pun: you will not build me a house, I will build you one. Your son gets the temple, your line gets the throne. Notably nobody mentions punishing that son when he goes wrong, which Samuel's version does. David sits down and prays some version of who am I for about twelve verses.
1CH 18:1-17	Highlight reel of conquest: Philistines, Moab, Hadadezer of Zobah all the way to the Euphrates, twenty-two thousand Syrians, Edom via Abishai. Everyone pays tribute. The line that gives the Chronicler away is the brass hauled out of Hadadezer's towns, which he notes Solomon later melted into the bronze sea and the pillars. Even the war loot is temple supplies. Tou of Hamath sends his son with gifts, David dedicates the lot, then the staff list.
1CH 19:1-19	David sends condolences to Ammon's new king and Ammon's advisers decide it is espionage. So they shave the envoys and cut their robes off at the backside, and David parks the men in Jericho until their beards grow back. That insult costs Ammon thirty-two thousand hired chariots. Joab finds himself boxed in front and rear, splits the army with his brother, and both halves win. The Syrian reinforcements lose their commander and quit the alliance for good.
1CH 20:1-8	Spring campaign, Joab besieges Rabbah, and the text says David stayed in Jerusalem. In Samuel that sentence is where Bathsheba happens. Here nothing happens: no roof, no affair, no Uriah, no Nathan. David just shows up for the crown, a talent of gold, and puts the city to work with saws and axes. Then giant killing. Sibbecai gets Sippai, Elhanan gets Goliath's brother Lahmi, and a nephew of David takes down the guy with twenty-four fingers and toes.
1CH 21:1-30	Samuel blamed God for the census. Chronicles blames Satan, which is the whole reason this version exists. Joab pushes back, gets overruled, then quietly refuses to count Levi and Benjamin. Plague takes seventy thousand, David sees an angel with a drawn sword over Jerusalem, and buys Ornan's threshing floor rather than accept it free: no burnt offering that costs nothing. Fire falls on the altar. Samuel priced the field at fifty shekels of silver; here it is six hundred in gold.
1CH 22:1-19	David looks at the threshing floor where the plague stopped and says right, this is the spot. Then he spends his remaining years buying the temple in advance: stonecutters, iron by the ton, brass nobody bothered weighing, cedar rafts from Tyre. He tells Solomon straight why the job is not his: too much blood on the hands. The kid's name means peace, and that is the point. A hundred thousand talents of gold later, he tells the princes to help.
1CH 23:1-32	David is old, makes Solomon king, and then spends the chapter on the org chart. Thirty-eight thousand Levites split into supervisors, judges, bouncers and a four thousand piece band using instruments David claims he designed. One line worth stopping on: Eleazar died with daughters and no sons, so their cousins married them and the family kept its slot. Also the tent is not going anywhere ever again, so service now starts at twenty instead of thirty.
1CH 24:1-31	Twenty-four priestly shifts, drawn by lot. Eleazar's side has twice as many big families as Ithamar's and could easily have taken the good weeks, but they draw instead, with a scribe recording it in front of everybody so nobody can argue later. Nadab and Abihu get one line: died before their dad, no kids. Course number eight is Abijah, which is worth remembering if you ever read the opening of Luke.
1CH 25:1-31	The worship band, and the text calls what they do prophesying. Two hundred and eighty-eight trained musicians under Asaph, Jeduthun and Heman. Heman had fourteen sons and three daughters, and the last stretch of his sons' names strings together into something that reads like a prayer chopped into people. Shifts assigned by lot, teacher against student, no seniority. Twenty-four groups of twelve, and that is the whole chapter.
1CH 26:1-32	Gatekeepers, and each gate is assigned by drawing lots rather than by rank. East, north, south, west, storehouse, guards posted facing each other. Obed-Edom shows up with sixty-two descendants and the text explains it flatly: God blessed him. That is the guy who housed the ark for three months. Hosah picked a non firstborn as chief and offers no reason. The temple treasury holds war loot dedicated by Samuel, Saul, Abner and Joab, which is a strange group photo.
1CH 27:1-34	The army runs on shifts too: twelve divisions, twenty-four thousand each, one month a year, so nobody's farm collapses. Asahel is listed as a captain even though he died young, with his son slotted in behind him. Then a very quiet confession: Joab started that census, never finished, wrath landed, and the total never made the official record. The chapter ends with David's ranch managers, right down to the guy in charge of the camels.
1CH 28:1-21	Big assembly, and David makes it official. He wanted to build the temple, God said no, too much blood on him, Solomon gets it instead. Then he hands over the blueprints, and these are not vibes: floor plan, courts, treasuries, exact weight of gold per lampstand, exact silver per bowl, even the cherubim. He claims the entire pattern arrived in writing from God, which is a very effective way to end an argument about the design.
1CH 29:1-14	David reminds everyone the kid is young and the job is enormous, then drops his own money on the pile: three thousand talents of Ophir gold and seven thousand of silver, on top of everything already stockpiled. Then he asks the room who is in. The princes and captains pile on gold, silver, brass, iron and gemstones, everybody is thrilled with themselves, and David prays a blessing that quietly points out none of this was ever theirs.
1CH 29:15-30	David keeps praying, and calls the whole crowd strangers whose days are a shadow, which lands oddly right after they donated a hundred thousand talents of iron. Everyone bows. Next day: a thousand bulls, a thousand rams, a thousand lambs, and Solomon gets crowned again, apparently for good measure, with Zadok installed as priest. David dies old and loaded after forty years on the throne, and the Chronicler points you at three seers' books for the rest.
1ES 1:1-29	First Esdras does not start at the start. It opens on Josiah's Passover, which tells you exactly what this book cares about. Same logistics Chronicles gives: thirty thousand lambs from the king's own flocks, Levites handling the catering so the gatekeepers never have to leave their gates, biggest Passover since Samuel. Then, with everything running perfectly, Josiah rides out to block an Egyptian army that does not want to fight him. This version says flatly that he ignored Jeremiah. Next time on 1 Esdras: Megiddo.
1ES 1:30-58	Josiah asks to be carried off the field, dies back home, and Jeremiah writes the lament that becomes a permanent national observance. Then four kings in quick succession, each one deposed or deported by whichever empire happens to be standing there. The officials who were supposed to guard the temple defile it, the messengers get laughed at, and Babylon finishes the job. The chapter ends on Jeremiah's arithmetic: seventy years of Sabbath owed to a country that never took any.
1ES 2:1-30	Cyrus' decree is bigger here than in Ezra: he announces that the Lord of Israel made him king of the entire world, which is quite the opening line. The vessel count is different too, 5,469 instead of 5,400, the sort of gap you get when two clerks count the same crates. Then the Samaria officials file their letter about the rebellious city and its tax-dodging future, the king checks the archives, the archives back them, and cavalry turn up to shut the site down.
1ES 3:1-24	This is the bit 1 Esdras exists for, and it is in no other book. Three of Darius's bodyguards, bored stiff after a state banquet, invent a debating contest with a prize list including purple robes, a gold bed and the title king's cousin, and slip their sealed entries under the sleeping king's pillow. Answers: wine, the king, and women plus truth. The wine argument is genuinely strong. It makes an orphan and a monarch identical, wipes out debt, and ends in swords nobody remembers drawing.
1ES 4:1-34	The second speech is the easy one: the king says kill and people kill, says build and people build, and he sleeps while everyone else stands guard all night. Then Zerubbabel takes women, moving from the reasonable, men leave their parents and homeland for a wife, to the frankly observational, thieves bring the loot home to impress her. His closing exhibit is having personally watched the king's concubine lift the crown off his head and slap him while he begged forgiveness. Next time on 1 Esdras: truth.
1ES 4:35-63	Zerubbabel pivots hard: heaven is high, the sun is fast, but bigger still is whoever made them, and truth beats all of it. Wine, kings, women, everybody, all unrighteous and all going to perish. Truth takes no bribes and does not die. The hall roars him the winner. Then, offered literally anything, he skips every prize on the list and reminds Darius of a vow he once made about Jerusalem, which is the shrewdest move in the book. He leaves with cedar, tax exemption and an annual budget.
1ES 5:1-29	Ezra sends the returning exiles off with a fast and a prayer. First Esdras sends them off with a thousand cavalry and a band playing tabrets and flutes, which is an entirely different mood for the same trip. Then the register, and if you hold Ezra 2 next to it the family totals drift by a few dozen here and there, being the same list translated twice. Four thousand priests, seventy-four Levites, and nobody ever explains that ratio. Next time on 1 Esdras: the ones who could not prove it.
1ES 5:30-50	Temple servants, and then the paragraph that shows up in every version of this list: families with no genealogy to produce, and priests struck off the register, one of them a man who married in and took his wife's family name instead of keeping his own. All barred from the holy things until a high priest with Urim and Thummim turns up. This version counts Israel from twelve up rather than twenty, and gives them seven thousand horses. Next time on 1 Esdras: altar first, temple later.
1ES 5:51-73	They start sacrificing on an altar sitting in open ground months before there is a building around it, which is the priority this entire book keeps hammering. Cedar comes down to Joppa on rafts, same route Solomon used. Then the foundation goes in, trumpets, cymbals, and the scene every telling of this story keeps: old men who remembered the first temple weeping so loudly nobody could hear the celebrating. The neighbours offer to help, get refused, and spend two years quietly sabotaging it.
1ES 6:1-19	Two prophets get the builders back on site, and a governor shows up with the usual two questions: who said you could, and give me the names of whoever is running this. Nobody gets arrested. Instead he writes to Darius, and his letter is genuinely fair minded, admiring how fast the work is going and quoting the Jews' own account in full, including the bit where they admit the first temple burned because their ancestors had it coming. Next time on 1 Esdras: someone checks the archives.
1ES 6:20-34	They search Babylon and the document turns up at Ekbatana, a palace in the Median hills, which is an extremely believable place for imperial paperwork to end up. Darius does not just say carry on. He orders the same governors who queried it to fund the build out of their own provincial tax take, supply the sacrificial animals daily, and use the proceeds to pray for him. Anyone obstructing gets a beam pulled from his own house and hanged on it.
1ES 7:1-15	The officials who came to investigate end up supervising the construction, which is the most satisfying turnaround in the book. Temple finished on a specific dated day in Darius's sixth year, credited jointly to the God of Israel and three Persian kings, an arrangement nobody here finds odd. A hundred bulls, two hundred rams, four hundred lambs, and twelve goats for twelve tribes, most of which no longer exist. Then Passover, and a week of unleavened bread.
1ES 8:1-29	Ezra turns up with a sixteen generation genealogy and a commission from Artaxerxes that is startlingly open handed: any Jew who wants to go can go, a hundred talents of silver available on request from provincial treasurers, total tax exemption for every temple employee, and authority to appoint judges across two provinces with the death penalty on the menu. Salt, quantity unspecified. The stated motive is that the king would rather not have wrath landing on his own realm. Next time on 1 Esdras: who signed up.
1ES 8:30-65	Ezra musters everyone at the river and spots the hole in his expedition: plenty of laymen, zero clergy. So he sends eleven men to a treasury town called Casiphia to go and ask for some, and comes back with thirty-eight Levites and 220 temple servants. Then he explains why there is no armed escort, and it is pure embarrassment: he had already told the king that God protects people who seek him, and could not then request cavalry. He weighs the treasure out, and weighs it back in. Next time on 1 Esdras: the news waiting for him.
1ES 8:66-96	The welcome he gets is the news that intermarriage is everywhere and the nobles led the way, at which Ezra tears not just his clothes but his priestly vestment, pulls out his own hair, and sits on the ground until evening. The prayer never once says they. It says our sins have piled up over our heads, and finishes by admitting he cannot stand there any longer. Then a crowd gathers, and a man proposes the remedy the book states without softening it: the wives and the children go.
1ES 9:1-28	The summons comes with real teeth: show up within three days or your livestock is confiscated to the temple and you are out of the community. They show up, and sit outside in weather so bad the text mentions it twice, which is how the assembly wins its one concession: you cannot settle this standing in the rain, so appoint commissioners and do it properly. Four men take the job on. Three months of hearings, and the list opens where it has to, with four priests from the high priest's own family. Next time on 1 Esdras: the rest of the names.
1ES 9:29-55	The list runs out, and the sentence closing it is stated flatly: they sent them away with their children. Then the book switches register completely. The people ask for the law themselves, nobody made them, and Ezra reads it from a wooden platform with thirteen men flanking him, dawn to noon, while Levites move through the crowd explaining as he goes. Everyone cries. And the last instruction in the book is go eat something rich, share it with anyone who has nothing, and stop being sad. Then it stops, mid sentence.
1MA 1:1-29	Alexander steamrolls Darius, takes basically everything, hands the empire out to his officers and dies twelve years in. His successors crown themselves and make things steadily worse. Then Antiochus Epiphanes. Some guys in Israel decide the whole covenant thing is cramping their style, build a gym in Jerusalem, and get surgery to undo their circumcision. Antiochus rolls Egypt, then robs the temple, literally scraping the gold off the front. Two years later: tax man, plus army. Next time on 1 Maccabees: he shows up smiling.
1MA 1:30-64	He arrives talking peace, then torches the city, loots it, and turns the old city of David into a fortress full of collaborators. Jerusalem empties out and the festivals become funerals. Then Antiochus makes it official: one empire, one people, one law, his. No sabbath, no offerings, swine on the altar, no circumcision, death for non-compliance. An abomination of desolation goes up on the altar in Chislev. Scrolls burned. Mothers executed with their babies. Plenty of people choose death over the menu.
1MA 2:1-34	Mattathias the priest bails on Jerusalem for Modin with five sons, including Judas, callsign Maccabaeus. Royal officials offer him cash and the title King's Friend if he'll sacrifice first and make it all look normal. He says no, for himself and everyone related to him. Then a local volunteers instead, and Mattathias kills him on the spot, kills the official too, and wrecks the altar. Off to the mountains with anyone who cares. Others hide in the wilderness and the army finds them on a Sabbath. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the caves answer.
1MA 2:35-70	They won't fight on the Sabbath, won't even barricade the entrances, and about a thousand die with their wives, kids and livestock. Mattathias makes the ruling: attacked on the Sabbath, we fight, otherwise there is nobody left to keep it. The Hasidaeans sign up. Altars come down, kids get circumcised whether the parents asked or not. On his deathbed Mattathias runs the greatest hits from Abraham to Daniel, names Simon the advisor and Judas the general, and dies. Buried at Modin.
1MA 3:1-31	Judas takes over and the text calls him a lion roaring over its kill. He drops Apollonius and keeps the man's sword for life, which is a whole personality. Seron turns up wanting a name for himself, meets Judas at the Bethhoron pass with a small hungry crew, and leaves eight hundred men on the ground. Antiochus loses it, pays the army a year up front, then opens the treasury and finds nothing, mostly his own fault, and heads east to shake down Persia. Next time on 1 Maccabees: he leaves a guy in charge.
1MA 3:32-60	Antiochus goes east and leaves Lysias half the army, the elephants and his son, with orders to wipe Israel out and move other people in. Lysias sends forty thousand infantry and seven thousand cavalry to Emmaus. Slave traders show up early with cash and chains, ready to buy prisoners nobody has captured yet. Judas responds with a fast at Mizpeh: sackcloth, ashes, the scroll open, the priestly vestments laid out. Then he sends home the newlyweds, the builders, the planters and anyone scared, per the rulebook.
1MA 4:1-33	Gorgias takes five thousand men out at night to jump Judas in his sleep, with informers from the citadel guiding him. Judas leaves by the other road and hits the empty camp at Emmaus, so Gorgias raids nothing. At dawn, three thousand under-equipped guys walk into the plain, get a two-line pep talk about the Red Sea, and wreck the army. No looting yet, Gorgias is still out there. Gorgias sees the smoke and runs. Next year Lysias brings sixty thousand. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Bethsura.
1MA 4:34-61	Lysias loses five thousand men and heads back to Antioch to hire more. Judas walks up to Mount Zion and finds the temple wrecked, gates burned, actual shrubbery growing in the courtyards. They take the defiled altar apart and stash the stones until a prophet shows up to say what to do with them, the most patient to-do item ever written. New altar, new gear, and on the twenty-fifth of Chislev, the exact anniversary of the desecration, eight days of harps and cymbals.
1MA 5:1-23	The neighbours take the rebuilt altar as a personal insult and start killing local Jews. Judas goes down the list: Esau's people at Akrabattine, the sons of Baean holed up in towers, Timotheus and the Ammonites. Then mail arrives from Jews trapped at Dathema, and while it's still being read, guys from Galilee turn up with their clothes ripped. Judas splits up: Simon takes three thousand north, he takes eight thousand east, Joseph and Azarias hold Judea with one instruction, do not fight. Next time on 1 Maccabees: across the Jordan.
1MA 5:24-44	Three days into the desert, friendly Nabathaeans hand Judas a list: these towns, these prisoners, all dead by tomorrow. He takes Bosora, marches all night, and arrives at the stronghold while the siege is in progress, hitting them from behind in three groups with trumpets. Eight thousand of Timotheus's men down. Timotheus regroups at Raphon with hired Arabians and a plan that depends entirely on Judas hesitating at the stream. Judas crosses first. Survivors hide in the temple at Carnaim. It burns. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the walk home.
1MA 5:45-68	Judas gathers every Jew in Gilead, wives, kids, cattle, all of it, for the trip back to Judah. Ephron blocks the only road and won't open the gates, so Judas removes Ephron from the map. They reach Mount Zion having lost nobody. Meanwhile Joseph and Azarias want their own headlines, ignore orders, attack Jamnia, and Gorgias kills two thousand of them. The narrator's verdict: wrong family. Judas then hits Hebron and Azotus, burns the idols, and goes home.
1MA 6:1-32	Antiochus tries to rob a rich temple in Elymais, the locals hear he is coming, and he gets run off. Then the mail catches up: Lysias beaten, the abomination torn down, the temple walled again. He goes to bed sick with grief and finally connects the dots, that maybe looting Jerusalem for no reason had consequences. He gives Philip the crown and dies. Lysias ignores that entirely and crowns the kid Eupator. The citadel complains, and the answer is a hundred thousand troops and thirty-two war elephants. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the elephants get a drink.
1MA 6:33-63	They get the elephants worked up on grape and mulberry juice, then hand each one a thousand armoured infantry, five hundred cavalry and a wooden tower full of guys. Sun hits the gold shields and the whole mountain lights up. Eleazar spots the biggest elephant, decides the king must be riding it, goes underneath and kills it, and it falls on him. The king was not on it. Bethsura and the temple both run out of food. Then Lysias hears Philip is grabbing the throne and instantly discovers he loves religious freedom. Peace sworn, wall demolished anyway.
1MA 7:1-24	Demetrius slips out of Rome, grabs a city on the coast, and the army delivers Antiochus and Lysias to him. He will not even look at them. Kills them. Alcimus wants the high priesthood, so he pulls the classic move and reports Judas to the king. Bacchides shows up with him and an army and offers peace, obviously lying. The Hasidaeans figure a real Aaronic priest would not hurt them. Sixty of them are dead the same day. Bacchides dumps more bodies in a pit and heads home. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Alcimus files another complaint.
1MA 7:25-50	Alcimus runs back to the king with more complaints and gets Nicanor, whose opening move is a friendly meeting that is actually an abduction. Judas clocks it. Nicanor loses five hundred men at Capharsalama, then stands in the temple mocking the priests who are literally sacrificing for his own king, and swears he will burn the building down. At Adasa on the thirteenth of Adar he is the first guy in his own army to die, and the rest drop their weapons on the spot. His head and that famous pointing right hand get hung up outside Jerusalem. Instant public holiday.
1MA 8:1-15	Judas gets a briefing on this Rome outfit and it is basically a highlight reel. Gaul: conquered. Spanish silver and gold mines: theirs. Philip and Perseus: handled. Antiochus the Great rolled up with a hundred and twenty elephants and they took him alive, put him on a payment plan, and made him hand over India, Media and Lydia. They hand out crowns and take them back. And nobody wears purple or a diadem, they just have three hundred and twenty guys sitting in a senate house every single day. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the pitch works.
1MA 8:16-32	The report finishes with the claim that the Romans give power to one guy for one year and nobody is jealous about it, which is a take. Judas sends Eupolemus and Jason on the extremely long trip to Rome anyway, and the senate signs. Terms go on brass tablets: we help you, you help us, and neither of us sells grain, weapons, cash or ships to the other's enemies. Renegotiate whenever. Then Rome writes Demetrius a letter asking why he is leaning on their friends, with war heavily implied.
1MA 9:1-24	Bacchides is back with twenty thousand infantry. Judas has three thousand at Elasa, and once everyone gets a look at the other side, he has eight hundred. His guys pitch a tactical retreat. Judas says he is not running and would rather die than leave a stain on his record, which is precisely what he gets. He smashes the right wing and chases it to Mount Azotus, the left wing swings around behind him, and Judas goes down. Buried at Modin. How is the mighty fallen. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the collaborators come out.
1MA 9:25-50	Bacchides hands the country to the collaborators and starts rounding up Judas's friends. Jonathan gets voted in as the new leader, which makes him the new target. Then the Jambri clan grabs his brother John and all his gear, so Jonathan and Simon stake out their wedding: bride from Nadabath, drums, musicians, ambush. The text says the marriage was turned into mourning. Later, trapped at the Jordan with water on both sides, Jonathan swings at Bacchides, Bacchides ducks, and everyone swims for it. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Alcimus picks up a sledgehammer.
1MA 9:51-73	Bacchides garrisons everything and takes the nobles' sons hostage. Alcimus decides to demolish the inner court wall, actual work of the prophets, and partway through the job he is struck down, loses his speech, and dies in agony without giving another order. Bacchides goes home. Two peaceful years. Then the collaborators talk him into coming back, he besieges Bethbasi, Jonathan sneaks out to hit the countryside, Simon torches the siege engines, and Bacchides loses. He kills the guys who suggested it, signs peace, hands back the prisoners, and never returns.
1MA 10:1-32	Bidding war. Alexander Epiphanes grabs Ptolemais and declares himself king, so Demetrius panics and writes Jonathan first: raise an army, have your hostages back. Jonathan immediately starts rebuilding Jerusalem's walls. Then Alexander outbids him, making Jonathan high priest and King's Friend with a purple robe and a gold crown. Jonathan puts on the vestments at Tabernacles. Demetrius counters with a letter cancelling tribute, the salt tax and the crown tax, declaring Jerusalem free, and throwing in the citadel. Next time on 1 Maccabees: he is not done offering.
1MA 10:33-58	The offer just keeps going: prisoners freed, tax breaks on every feast day, thirty thousand Jewish troops on the payroll under their own officers, three Samaritan districts annexed, Ptolemais deeded to the temple, and the crown covering the construction bill. Jonathan reads it, remembers who Demetrius actually is, and passes. They stick with Alexander, who asked nicely first. Battle time: Alexander's army runs, Alexander wins anyway, because Demetrius dies in it. He marries Ptolemy's daughter Cleopatra. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Jonathan gets an invite.
1MA 10:59-89	Alexander calls Jonathan to Ptolemais, puts him in purple, sits him next to the throne, and has a herald announce that complaints about this man are now illegal. The complainers leave immediately. Then Demetrius's son shows up from Crete, and his general Apollonius sends pure trash talk: come down from your hills and fight in the plain, your fathers ran twice. Jonathan takes Joppa, then at Azotus just stands there all day letting the hidden cavalry throw darts until the horses are wrecked, and Simon walks through the infantry. Eight thousand dead. Jonathan gets Ekron.
1MA 11:1-26	Ptolemy of Egypt rolls into Syria talking peace and drops a garrison in every city he passes, which is annexation with extra steps. At Azotus the locals literally pile the burned bodies in his path to complain about Jonathan. He says nothing. Then he takes his daughter back off Alexander, marries her to Demetrius instead, and wears both crowns at once. Alexander loses, runs to Arabia, and his host cuts his head off and mails it. Ptolemy dies three days later. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Jonathan turns up with gifts.
1MA 11:27-48	Demetrius confirms Jonathan as high priest and sells him tax exemption for three hundred talents, in writing, posted on the holy mount so it cannot be quietly forgotten. Then, feeling safe, he lays off the army that put him on the throne, and the army takes it badly. Tryphon goes to fetch Alexander's little kid as a replacement king. Jonathan asks him to clear the citadel and gets asked for troops instead. His three thousand guys end up saving Demetrius when a hundred and twenty thousand Antiochenes try to kill him. Next time on 1 Maccabees: gratitude, allegedly.
1MA 11:49-74	Antioch caves, and the Jews go home rich and popular. Demetrius, now safe, immediately breaks every promise, and the narrator says flatly that he lied in everything he said. Tryphon shows up with Alexander's kid, hoovers up the troops Demetrius fired, and takes Antioch plus the elephants. Boy king confirms Jonathan as high priest and hands Simon the whole coast. Gaza locks him out and gets its suburbs torched until it folds. Then at Hazor an ambush scatters his entire army except two guys, so Jonathan throws dirt on his head, prays, turns around, and wins.
1MA 12:1-28	Jonathan renews the treaty with Rome and, while he is at it, writes to Sparta, based on an old letter claiming Spartans and Jews are actually related, both from Abraham. The letter is beautifully passive: we don't need anything, we have the scriptures, but we would hate to lose touch, and by the way we mention you at our sacrifices. Then Demetrius's generals come back with a bigger army. Spies say night attack. Jonathan keeps everyone armed and awake with sentries out. Next time on 1 Maccabees: check the fires.
1MA 12:29-53	They leave the campfires burning and sneak off, and Jonathan does not notice till morning. He chases, misses, and takes it out on some Arabs instead. Simon grabs Joppa before Demetrius's people can. They build the walls higher and wall the citadel off so nobody can even trade with it. Then Tryphon, who wants the crown and sees Jonathan as the obstacle, is extremely friendly at Bethshan, says forty thousand troops is a lot between friends, and invites him to Ptolemais with a thousand. Gates shut. Jonathan taken. Israel mourns.
1MA 13:1-29	Simon gets up in Jerusalem, points out his entire family has died doing this and he is the only one left, and offers to die too. Crowd makes him leader immediately. Tryphon offers to sell Jonathan back for a hundred talents and two of his sons. Simon knows it is a scam, pays anyway so nobody can blame him later. Tryphon takes the money and the kids and keeps Jonathan. Then a blizzard blocks his route to the citadel, so he kills Jonathan in Gilead. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Tryphon promotes himself.
1MA 13:30-53	Tryphon murders the kid he was supposedly ruling for and crowns himself. Simon sends Demetrius a gold crown and a palm branch and gets back tax immunity, a full amnesty, and legal title to his own forts. The occupation is over, and people start dating contracts by year one of Simon, which is how you know it stuck. Gazara gets a siege engine and a deep clean. The citadel starves out and surrenders, and Simon walks in with harps, cymbals and palm branches. New annual holiday. His son John gets the army.
1MA 14:1-27	Demetrius goes to Media looking for reinforcements and gets arrested the moment he arrives. Then the book takes a breath and just describes peace: Joppa working as a port, crops coming in, old men chatting in the street, young guys in nice armour, everybody under their own vine and fig tree with nobody to scare them. Rome and Sparta hear Jonathan is dead and write to Simon on brass to renew the deal. Simon sends Rome a solid gold shield weighing a thousand pounds. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the plaque.
1MA 14:28-49	Here is the actual honours decree, read out at Asaramel and engraved on brass. It runs through the family record, notes Simon armed the troops with his own money, then gets to the point: leader and high priest forever, or until a faithful prophet shows up to say otherwise. That clause is doing a lot of work. Nobody may argue with him, call a meeting without him, or wear purple. Copies filed in the treasury for Simon and his sons, obviously.
1MA 15:1-22	Antiochus son of Demetrius writes from offshore offering Simon everything he already owns, plus permission to mint his own coins, which is the only genuinely new item. He lands, the whole army switches sides, and Tryphon gets pinned at Dor by land and sea. Meanwhile Numenius gets back from Rome with a form letter sent to every king in the neighbourhood: leave the Jews alone, hand over their fugitives, and thanks for the shield, we kept it. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the offer gets withdrawn.
1MA 15:23-41	The Roman letter goes to basically every port in the Mediterranean: Sparta, Delos, Rhodes, Cyprus, Cyrene, on and on. Simon sends Antiochus two thousand troops and a pile of silver and gold. Antiochus refuses the help and cancels every deal they ever had. His envoy Athenobius demands Joppa, Gazara and the citadel or a thousand talents, then stands in Simon's house gawping at the gold dinnerware. Simon says they took nothing that was not their inheritance, offers a hundred talents, and Athenobius leaves furious. Cendebaeus starts raiding Judea from Kidron.
1MA 16:1-24	John rides in with news of the raids, and Simon, too old to fight, hands the army to his sons with a short speech about the family trade. The men balk at the brook, John crosses first, everyone follows, Cendebaeus is finished. Then it goes dark. Ptolemy son of Abubus, governor of Jericho and Simon's own son in law, throws Simon and two of his sons a banquet at Dok, waits for the drink to land, and murders them. He sends killers to Gazara for John, who is warned and kills them first. The book just stops there.
2CH 1:1-17	Kings just says Solomon had a dream at Gibeon and moves on. The Chronicler wants you to know why he was there: the actual Tent of Meeting and Bezalel's bronze altar were parked at Gibeon, so this is not sketchy worship, it is the right place. A thousand burnt offerings, then God says name it. Solomon asks for wisdom to handle the job and gets wisdom plus all the money and honour he did not ask for. Then he starts stockpiling chariots and horses.
2CH 2:1-18	Solomon writes to Huram of Tyre with an order and a disclaimer: this house will be enormous, though obviously heaven cannot hold God, so really it is just a place to burn incense. He wants cedar, cypress, algum and one seriously good metalworker, paying in wheat, barley, wine and oil. Huram writes back praising Israel's God, which is excellent client management, and sends a craftsman whose mother was from Dan. The labour is 153,600 resident foreigners, and the text is careful to say so.
2CH 3:1-17	One word Kings never drops: Moriah. Chronicles names the temple hill as the mountain, not just Ornan's threshing floor, then moves briskly on to dimensions. Sixty by twenty, porch out front, cypress ceilings, gold on absolutely everything, six hundred talents of it in the inner room alone. Somebody weighed the nails: fifty shekels of gold. Two carved cherubim with a combined twenty cubit wingspan, and the two front pillars get names, Jachin and Boaz.
2CH 4:1-22	Fittings day. A bronze altar twenty cubits square that Kings never bothered to mention, and the big cast sea sitting on twelve oxen, three per compass point, all facing out. Capacity three thousand baths here, two thousand in Kings, and nobody addresses the discrepancy. Ten lampstands, ten tables, a hundred gold bowls. Huram cast the bronze out in the Jordan clay flats, and there was so much of it the text gives up on weighing.
2CH 5:1-14	Ark goes in under the cherubim wings, poles deliberately long enough to still be visible from the front. And after chapters of gold by the talent, the narrator mentions the box itself contains two stone tablets and nothing else. Then Chronicles adds the bit Kings skips: Asaph, Heman and Jeduthun's whole choir in linen, a hundred and twenty trumpeters, everyone hitting one sound at once. The cloud drops and the priests physically cannot stay standing.
2CH 6:1-22	Solomon starts with God said he lives in thick darkness, so I built him a house, and just keeps going. Chronicles adds a detail Kings leaves out: a purpose-built bronze platform, five cubits square, so the whole assembly can watch the king kneel. The prayer itself admits the obvious, that the heavens cannot hold God and this building definitely cannot, which is a bold thing to say about your own seven year construction project while standing in it.
2CH 6:23-42	Solomon works through the disaster list in advance: lawsuits, defeat, drought, famine, blight, locusts, siege, any plague you like. He asks that foreigners who travel in for the name get their prayers answered too, explicitly so word gets around. And the scenario he spends the most words on is exile: carried off, coming to their senses, praying toward home. Given who was reading Chronicles after the exile, that is not a hypothetical.
2CH 7:1-22	Kings ends the prayer with a cloud. Chronicles adds fire falling from heaven and eating the offering while the entire nation watches face down on the pavement. Then the barbecue math: twenty-two thousand cattle, a hundred and twenty thousand sheep, so much that the brand new bronze altar cannot keep up and they have to consecrate the courtyard as overflow. God shows up that night with the humble yourselves and I will heal your land line, then adds that this building can absolutely be demolished.
2CH 8:1-18	Small but pointed edit: Kings said Solomon paid Hiram in towns. Chronicles says Huram gave cities to Solomon, who rebuilt them and moved Israelites in. Otherwise it is twenty years of admin, fortresses, store cities, chariot towns, with forced labour drawn strictly from the leftover Canaanites and a firm note that no Israelite was a slave. Pharaoh's daughter gets moved out of David's old house because the ark made it holy, which is as close as this book gets to Solomon's marriages.
2CH 9:1-31	Sheba's queen turns up with a caravan and a list of hard questions, gets through all of them, then looks at the food, the staff and the stairway to the temple and basically loses the ability to speak. She admits the rumours undersold him. Then wealth stats until the numbers stop registering: 666 talents a year, gold shields, an ivory throne with fourteen lions, silver treated as worthless. Then he dies. No foreign wives, no idols, no downfall. Chronicles just does not go there.
2CH 10:1-19	North comes to Shechem asking for lighter taxes. Rehoboam asks his father's old advisers, who say be nice to them and they will follow you forever. Then he asks his mates, who workshop the line about his little finger being thicker than dad's waist, and he uses it. Ten tribes walk. He then sends the head of the forced labour department to smooth things over, and they stone the man to death. Rehoboam gets in his chariot and leaves quickly.
2CH 11:1-23	Rehoboam musters 180,000 troops to take the north back, a prophet says do not fight your brothers, and he actually listens. So he builds walls instead: fifteen fortified towns stocked with food, oil, wine and weapons. Meanwhile the priests and Levites pack up and move south, because Jeroboam fired them and staffed his calf shrines with whoever showed up. Then eighteen wives, sixty concubines, eighty-eight kids, and the sons parcelled out to garrison towns, which the text calls dealing wisely.
2CH 12:1-16	The moment Rehoboam feels secure he drops the law, and Chronicles files the consequence right underneath. Shishak of Egypt rolls in with twelve hundred chariots and takes the fortified cities. Shemaiah delivers the memo, the king and his princes admit God is righteous, and the sentence is commuted from wiped out to working for somebody else. Shishak walks off with Solomon's gold shields. Rehoboam replaces them with bronze and has the guards lock them up between uses, which says everything.
2CH 13:1-22	Abijah is outnumbered two to one and responds by giving a speech at the enemy from a hilltop. Covenant of salt, Jeroboam the runaway servant, and the northern priesthood you can join by turning up with a bull and seven rams. Jeroboam's reply is an ambush from behind. Judah, boxed in, yells, the priests blow the trumpets, and the north loses half a million men. Abijah takes Bethel off him, Jeroboam never recovers, and Abijah goes home to fourteen wives.
2CH 14:1-15	Asa gets ten quiet years and spends them tearing down shrines and putting up walls, on the logic that peace is build time, not downtime. Then Zerah the Ethiopian shows up with a million men and three hundred chariots. Asa's prayer is short and good: it makes no difference to you whether we are strong or have nothing. Judah wins, chases them to Gerar, and comes home with sheep, camels and enough loot to fund the next chapter.
2CH 15:1-19	A prophet catches Asa coming back from the battle with the line this whole book runs on: seek him and he is there, ditch him and he is gone. Asa takes it to heart, clears out the idols, and throws a national covenant ceremony with trumpets, shouting, seven hundred cattle and a clause making it a capital offence not to seek God. He also fires his own grandmother from the queen mother job and burns her idol at the Kidron.
2CH 16:1-14	Thirty-five years of trusting God, then Baasha builds one fort and Asa panics, raids the temple treasury and pays Syria to switch sides. It works. That is exactly the problem. Hanani points out that the guy who beat a million Ethiopians just bought a smaller victory on credit, and Asa throws him in prison. Later his feet fail badly and he sees only doctors, never God. Great funeral though, spices, perfumers, an enormous fire.
2CH 17:1-19	Jehoshaphat starts with garrisons and then does something genuinely unusual for a king in this book: he sends a teaching tour round Judah, five princes, nine Levites, two priests, carrying the actual law book, going town to town. Everybody nearby gets nervous and stops attacking. Philistines send tribute, Arabians send 7,700 rams and 7,700 goats. Then the muster: over a million men, and one commander is singled out for just volunteering himself.
2CH 18:1-16	Kings runs this story as Ahab's. Chronicles runs it as Jehoshaphat's cautionary tale about who you have dinner with. Four hundred prophets say yes go, all of them, and one has built iron horns as a prop. Jehoshaphat politely asks if there is maybe one more prophet available. There is, and Ahab openly says he hates the guy for never telling him what he wants to hear. Next time on 2 Chronicles: Micaiah stops being polite.
2CH 18:17-34	Micaiah's real answer is a scene in heaven: God asking who will lure Ahab to his death, and a spirit stepping up to be a lie in four hundred mouths. He gets slapped for it and jailed on bread and water until the king returns safe, which he points out is the entire question. Ahab then fights in disguise and asks Jehoshaphat to wear the robes, thanks pal. One arrow, fired at nothing in particular, finds the seam in his armour.
2CH 19:1-11	A seer is waiting at the gate when Jehoshaphat gets home: nice alliance, do you normally help people who hate God? Then adds that there are still good things in him, which is a mixed review. Jehoshaphat's fix is bureaucracy. Judges installed in every fortified city, told flat out they are ruling for God not for people, no bribes, no favourites. Jerusalem gets an appeals bench, priests for religious cases, a royal officer for the king's.
2CH 20:1-19	Three armies incoming, already at En Gedi, and Jehoshaphat's first move is a national fast. His prayer quotes Solomon's temple dedication straight back at God, then finishes with the most honest line in the book: we have no power, we have no idea what to do, we are looking at you. A Levite from Asaph's choir gets the answer, and it is basically show up and watch. Next time on 2 Chronicles: the singers lead the charge.
2CH 20:20-37	Jehoshaphat puts the choir in front of the army. Not the shields, the choir. The moment they start singing, the invading coalition turns on itself, Ammon and Moab wipe out Seir and then each other, and Judah walks up to a field of bodies and three days of loot they cannot carry. They name the valley Blessing and go home with harps. Later he goes into shipping with the king of Israel, gets warned, and the fleet is wrecked before it sails.
2CH 21:1-20	Jehoshaphat leaves the other sons money and towns and the crown to Jehoram, who tidies up by murdering all six brothers. He marries Ahab's daughter, imports her religion, and loses Edom and Libnah on the way. Then a letter turns up from Elijah, who in Kings never has anything to do with Judah, forecasting a bowel disease. It takes two years and it is exactly as bad as advertised. Raiders take his wives and sons. Nobody lit a fire for him. Nobody missed him.
2CH 22:1-12	Ahaziah only gets the throne because raiders killed every older brother, and his mother Athaliah runs his policy, which the text says outright was the whole problem. He picks the worst possible day to visit his wounded cousin in Jezreel: Jehu is in town wiping out Ahab's family. They kill him anyway, then bury him properly because his grandfather was Jehoshaphat. Athaliah kills the rest of the royal line, missing one baby his aunt smuggled into the temple for six years.
2CH 23:1-21	Jehoiada runs the coup like a rota. Five captains sworn in, Levites bussed in from every town, split into three shifts by gate, and armed from storage with King David's actual spears and shields. Then they crown the seven year old in the middle of the temple. Athaliah wanders in, sees the trumpets and the crowd, manages to shout treason twice, and gets escorted outside to die, because the priest is not having a killing in the building.
2CH 24:1-27	Joash does great, right up until the man who raised him dies. The temple restoration works once they give up on the Levites collecting and just put a box by the gate; the money comes in faster than they can spend it. Then Jehoiada dies at 130, the princes turn up flattering, and the idols come straight back. Jehoiada's own son calls it out and Joash has him stoned in the temple court. A tiny Syrian raiding party then routs Judah, and his own staff kill him in bed.
2CH 25:1-14	Amaziah kills his father's assassins but spares their kids, quoting Moses on nobody dying for someone else's sin, which is a good look. Then he hires 100,000 Israelite mercenaries, a prophet says send them home, and his only concern is the hundred talents he already paid. Told God can cover it, he sends them off, and they trash Judean towns on the way out. He wins in Edom, throws ten thousand prisoners off a cliff, and brings the losers' gods home to worship. Next time on 2 Chronicles: somebody asks him why.
2CH 25:15-28	The prophet's question is a killer: why are you worshipping gods that could not save their own people from you? Amaziah's answer is do you work here. Then, high on beating Edom, he challenges Israel, and Joash replies with a fable about a thistle asking a cedar for its daughter's hand and getting stepped on by a passing animal. Amaziah goes anyway, gets captured, and watches 400 cubits of his own wall come down. His own people later chase him to Lachish and kill him.
2CH 26:1-23	Uzziah is spectacularly good at this. Philistine walls flattened, tribute from Ammon, towers, cisterns, vineyards, farms he ran personally because he loved farming, an army of 307,500, and catapults on the battlements for shooting stones. Then one sentence flips it: when he was strong, his heart was lifted up. He marches into the sanctuary with a censer, eighty priests block the door, and leprosy breaks out on his forehead mid argument. He lives alone in a separate house for the rest of his life.
2CH 27:1-9	Sixteen years, and the detail that matters is one clause: he did right, but he did not go into the temple. After watching what happened to his dad, that is not devotion, that is a man who learned exactly one lesson very well. Otherwise it is gates, walls, forts in the forest, and three years running of Ammonite tribute in silver, wheat and barley. The people stayed corrupt regardless, which the text notes without further comment.
2CH 28:1-27	Ahaz makes Baal idols and burns his own children in the valley of Hinnom, and the disasters queue up. Syria takes prisoners, Pekah kills 120,000 Judeans in a day, 200,000 women and kids get marched north. Then the best scene in the chapter happens in Israel: a prophet and four Ephraimite chiefs stop the column, and the victors clothe, feed and anoint the captives and carry the weak home on donkeys. Ahaz pays Assyria for help he never gets, then worships Damascus's gods and locks the temple doors.
2CH 29:1-18	Hezekiah takes the throne at twenty-five and his very first move, first month, first year, is unlocking the temple his father bolted shut. His speech to the Levites does not soften anything: the lamps went out, the offerings stopped, and that is exactly why your families are in captivity right now. Then sixteen days of the least glamorous job in Chronicles, hauling accumulated filth out of the holy place and dumping it in the Kidron. Next time on 2 Chronicles: they find what Ahaz threw out.
2CH 29:19-36	The vessels Ahaz chucked turn up cleaned and laid out by the altar, which is when the restoration stops being a speech. Seven bulls, rams, lambs and goats as a sin offering for all Israel, from a king who rules none of the north. The music is cued exactly: offering starts, song starts, trumpets and David's instruments together. One awkward line in the accounts: there were not enough consecrated priests to skin the animals, so the Levites did it, being more diligent about consecrating themselves.
2CH 30:1-27	Hezekiah invites the whole north to Passover and moves the date a month because nobody was ready on time. Most of Ephraim laughs the couriers out of town. A few from Asher, Manasseh and Zebulun turn up anyway. Then the actual issue: half the crowd is ritually unclean and eats the Passover regardless, and Hezekiah just prays that God pardon anyone whose heart is in it, clean or not. God says fine. They have such a good time they run it a second week.
2CH 31:1-21	Everyone leaves the festival and goes out smashing pillars and high places, including in Ephraim and Manasseh, where Hezekiah has no authority whatsoever. Then the boring part that actually matters: divisions restored, the king funding the daily offerings out of his own pocket, and tithes arriving in such quantity they sit in literal heaps for four months. Hezekiah asks what the heaps are and the chief priest says we have all eaten our fill and this is the leftovers. So they build storerooms.
2CH 32:1-17	Sennacherib invades and Hezekiah gets practical fast: plug every spring outside the city so the besieging army turns up thirsty, patch the wall, build a second wall behind it, mass produce weapons. Then a decent pep talk, the good line being that Assyria has an arm of flesh and we have God. Sennacherib writes back with a list of every god who failed to save his people, plus a genuinely sly jab: your own king tore down your altars. Next time on 2 Chronicles: the mail gets answered.
2CH 32:18-33	The Assyrians shout their propaganda in Hebrew at the men on the wall, which is the detail that tells you how sieges actually worked. Hezekiah and Isaiah pray. An angel goes through the Assyrian camp, and Sennacherib goes home in disgrace and gets stabbed by his own sons in his god's temple. Then Chronicles speedruns the rest: fatal illness, sign, got proud, climbed down, Babylonian envoys sent to see what was really in his heart. Also he rerouted the Gihon spring.
2CH 33:1-25	Fifty-five years of the worst king Judah ever had: altars to the star gods in the temple courts, an idol inside the sanctuary, his own children burned in Hinnom, mediums, sorcery, the lot. Kings stops there. Chronicles keeps going: Assyrian officers drag him to Babylon in fetters, and in prison he prays, and God actually listens, and he comes home and demolishes everything he spent his reign building. Then Amon does all the same sins and skips the repentance, and his own staff kill him.
2CH 34:1-17	Josiah is crowned at eight, starts seeking God at sixteen, starts swinging hammers at twenty. The demolition is personal: idols ground to actual powder, powder sprinkled over the graves of the people who worshipped them, and the bones of their priests burned on their own altars. He takes the tour as far north as Naphtali. Then, four years later, during what is basically a construction budget audit, Hilkiah mentions he has found the book of the law lying around in the temple. Next time on 2 Chronicles: someone actually reads it to him.
2CH 34:18-33	Somebody reads the law out loud to the king and he rips his clothes, which tells you exactly how long that book had been missing. His officials go to Huldah, a prophetess living in Jerusalem's second district, and she gives it straight in two parts: the curses are coming and nothing stops them, and Josiah will be dead before he has to watch. His response is to read the entire book to the whole country himself and swear the covenant anyway.
2CH 35:1-27	Biggest Passover since Samuel, and the logistics are the story: thirty thousand lambs from the king's own flocks, princes chipping in thousands more, Levites doing the flaying because the priests are working till nightfall, the choir never leaving their spots because colleagues bring them food. Then, with everything finally right, Josiah rides out to block an Egyptian army that explicitly does not want to fight him, ignores a pharaoh quoting God at him, and takes an arrow at Megiddo.
2CH 36:1-23	Four kings in twenty-three years. Egypt deposes one and renames his replacement. Babylon fetters the next, deports the one after that, and installs Zedekiah, who breaks his oath and will not listen to Jeremiah. God keeps sending messengers, they keep getting laughed at, until the text says flatly there was no remedy. Temple burned, walls down, everyone deported. And the odd upside: the land finally gets seventy years of Sabbath. Then Cyrus says go home and build, mid sentence, end of book.
2JN 1:1-13	Short note, mostly about who is not welcome. The elder says hi to a chosen lady, is happy her kids are doing well, and reminds everyone about the loving one another thing, which he stresses is not a new rule. Then the point: there are guys going around claiming Jesus never actually came in a body. If one shows up, don't let him in, don't even say hello, because saying hello makes you a co-conspirator. Rest in person.
2MA 1:1-18	The book starts with mail. Jerusalem writes to the Jews in Egypt: we pray for you, remember when Jason wrecked everything, please observe the Chislev festival. The second letter has the news. Antiochus turned up at the temple of Nanaea in Persia claiming he wanted to marry the goddess, purely so he could pocket her treasure as the dowry. The priests let him in, shut the doors, opened a hatch in the ceiling and dropped rocks on him. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the fire that turned into water.
2MA 1:19-36	Where the festival comes from. Before the exile the priests hid the altar fire down a dry well. Generations later Nehemiah sends their descendants to go get it, and what they find is not fire but thick water. He pours it on the sacrifice anyway, the sun comes out, and the whole altar catches. Prayers, hymns, and the leftover liquid sets stones on fire as a bonus. The Persian king fences the site off and starts gifting the stuff to favourites. They call it Nephthar. Everyone else says Nephthai.
2MA 2:1-17	Turns out Jeremiah hid the ark. God tipped him off, so he hauled the tabernacle, the ark and the incense altar up the mountain Moses climbed, sealed them in a cave in the rock, and when guys tried to mark the trail he told them off and said the spot stays lost until God gathers the people. Then a quick list of times fire fell from heaven, a note that Nehemiah founded a library, and that Judas collected the books scattered by the war. Need copies? Send a courier. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the author introduces himself.
2MA 2:18-32	Letters over. Now the actual author steps up: everything from here is five books by Jason of Cyrene squeezed into one, because nobody was getting through five. He wants it readable and memorable. He compares the original historian to the guy who builds the house and himself to the guy who paints it, complains that abridging is sweaty thankless work like catering a party, then says a long preface to a short history is stupid and stops.
2MA 3:1-19	Under high priest Onias, Jerusalem runs so smoothly the king personally funds the sacrifices. Then Simon, who runs temple security, loses a turf war over the city market and gets even by telling the governor the treasury is stuffed with cash. Enter Heliodorus, sent to take it. Onias explains it is deposits: widows, orphans and one extremely connected man named Hyrcanus, four hundred talents of silver and two hundred of gold. Heliodorus does not care. City panics, priests hit the floor, women in sackcloth flood the streets. Next time on 2 Maccabees: a horse.
2MA 3:20-40	Heliodorus walks into the treasury and gets met by a horse. Golden armoured rider, front hooves to the chest, plus two gorgeous young men who appear on either side and whip him thoroughly. He drops, blacks out, and gets carried out on a stretcher, having walked in with a full honour guard. His friends beg Onias to pray for him, and Onias, mostly worried the king will suspect foul play, does it. Heliodorus lives, tells everyone, and advises the king that if he has an enemy he wants beaten, send him to Jerusalem.
2MA 4:1-25	Simon, who caused all of this, now accuses Onias of causing it, people start turning up murdered, and Onias goes to the king. Seleucus dies, Antiochus Epiphanes takes over, and Jason buys the high priesthood off his own brother for three hundred and sixty talents, plus extra for a licence to build a gym and register everyone in Jerusalem as citizens of Antioch. Priests start skipping the altar to go throw the discus. Then Menelaus, sent to deliver Jason's payment, outbids him by three hundred talents. Next time on 2 Maccabees: nobody pays.
2MA 4:26-50	Jason, who stole the job from his brother, gets it stolen from him and runs to the Ammonites. Menelaus pays the king exactly nothing, which gets noticed. He bribes the deputy Andronicus with stolen temple gold, and when Onias calls it out from sanctuary, Andronicus swears friendship, talks him out of the building and kills him. Even the Greeks are disgusted. Antiochus cries, rips off Andronicus's purple and executes him on the exact spot. Then Lysimachus loots the temple and a mob with rocks, logs and fistfuls of ash kills him by the treasury. Menelaus bribes his way clear.
2MA 5:1-14	For nearly forty days there is cavalry in the sky over Jerusalem, gold robes, drawn swords, charges and countercharges, and everyone hopes it means something good. It does not. Somebody says Antiochus is dead, so Jason attacks the city with a thousand men and massacres his own people, and the narrator notes that beating your own countrymen is the worst kind of win. He gets nothing out of it and dies a hunted exile with no funeral. Antiochus, assuming revolt, takes the city and spends three days killing. Eighty thousand dead, as many sold. Next time on 2 Maccabees: he goes inside.
2MA 5:15-27	Menelaus personally gives Antiochus the tour so he can take the sacred vessels and every gift earlier kings ever donated, eighteen hundred talents of it. The narrator is careful about it: God looked away a while because of the nation's sins, and the temple exists for the people, not the other way round. Then Apollonius shows up with twenty-two thousand men, acts friendly, waits for the Sabbath, parades his soldiers, and kills everyone who came out to watch. Judas and about nine others take to the mountains and live on whatever grows there.
2MA 6:1-17	An old man from Athens turns up with orders to abolish the law and rename the temple after Jupiter Olympius, and the place is turned into a nonstop party. No Sabbath, no feasts, and you cannot even say out loud that you are a Jew. Monthly compulsory sacrificial meat for the king's birthday, plus an ivy-wreath parade for Bacchus. Two women who had their sons circumcised are thrown off the wall with their babies. People hiding in caves to keep the Sabbath burn rather than fight back. The author pauses to say this is chastening, not abandonment. Next time on 2 Maccabees: Eleazar refuses.
2MA 6:18-31	Eleazar is ninety, a senior scribe, and they try to make him eat pork. He spits it out and walks to the torture himself. His old friends running the ceremony offer him an out: bring your own lawful meat, we will pretend you complied. He says no, because the young would hear that Eleazar caved at ninety, and because God is not fooled by staging. Under the scourge he says plainly that he could have avoided this and is choosing it. The text keeps his death as the example he intended.
2MA 7:1-21	Seven brothers and their mother are arrested and whipped to force them to eat pork. The oldest speaks for all of them and refuses, and the king has him mutilated and burned alive in a pan while the rest watch. The second is scalped and says God will raise them again. The third puts out his tongue and hands and says heaven gave them and heaven will give them back. One after another the brothers die refusing. Their mother watches all of it in a single day. No joke belongs here. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the youngest son.
2MA 7:22-42	The mother tells her sons she did not give them their breath and the Creator who did will give it back. Antiochus, convinced he is being laughed at, offers the youngest boy money, rank and his personal friendship, then asks the mother to talk him round. She says yes, leans in, and in their own language tells him to look at the sky and the earth, know that God made all of it out of nothing, and not to be afraid of the butcher. The boy refuses, warns the king he will not escape God, and dies. His mother dies last.
2MA 8:1-19	Judas rounds up six thousand guys from the villages and starts hitting at night, torching towns and taking positions back until everyone knows his name. Philip writes for backup, and Nicanor turns up with twenty thousand men and a business plan: pay off the king's two thousand talent debt to Rome by selling Jews, ninety slaves per talent, dealers invited ahead of time. The nervous ones desert. Everyone else sells what they own and prays for the ones already sold. Judas notes the other side trusts weapons. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the buyers' money changes hands.
2MA 8:20-36	Watchword: The Help of God. Judas splits the army four ways under himself and his brothers, reads the scroll out loud, and goes. Nine thousand of Nicanor's men dead, the rest running, and the Jews pocket the cash the slave traders brought to buy them, which is deeply satisfying bookkeeping. They stop early because the Sabbath is starting, then split the spoil with the disabled, the widows and the orphans. Twenty thousand more of Timotheus's men later. Nicanor slinks back to Antioch in disguise telling everyone the Jews have somebody fighting for them.
2MA 9:1-13	Antiochus gets run out of Persepolis for trying to rob a temple, hears what happened to Nicanor, and decides to take it out on Jerusalem, announcing he will turn it into a mass grave. He barely finishes the sentence before his insides seize up. He tells the driver to go faster anyway, falls out of the moving chariot, and finishes the trip in a litter while worms come out of him and the smell drives off his own army. The guy who thought he could weigh mountains decides mortals should not be arrogant. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the apology letter.
2MA 9:14-29	The bargaining starts. Jerusalem goes free, the Jews get citizenship equal to Athens, the temple gets refurnished on his own account, and, top offer, he will personally become a Jew and tour the world advertising God's power. It does not work. So he writes the Jews a letter addressed to fellow citizens, in the breezy tone of a man with a cold, and gets to the actual point: naming his son king before the neighbours spot the gap. He dies abroad, and Philip takes the body home and runs to Egypt.
2MA 10:1-19	Judas takes the temple back, tears down the foreign altars in the marketplace, and strikes fresh fire out of stones for a new one. Sacrifices restart after a two-year gap on the twenty-fifth of Chislev, the exact anniversary of the desecration, and run eight days with palm branches like tabernacles, because last tabernacles they spent in caves. Then the politics: Eupator takes over, Lysias runs things, and Ptolemy Macron gets branded a traitor for treating Jews fairly and poisons himself. Gorgias hires mercenaries. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the two towers.
2MA 10:20-38	Some of Simon's guys take a seventy thousand drachma bribe to let besieged men slip away, and Judas has them executed for selling out their own people, then takes the towers regardless. Timotheus comes back with cavalry from across Asia. After prayers in sackcloth and dirt, five horsemen with gold bridles appear out of the sky, two riding either side of Judas as personal armour while firing arrows and thunderbolts at the other side. Twenty thousand five hundred dead. Timotheus hides in Gazara, then in a cistern. Found.
2MA 11:1-18	Lysias shows up with eighty thousand infantry and eighty elephants, planning to settle Greeks in Jerusalem, tax the temple like any other shrine, and auction off the high priesthood every single year, which is at least honest. The Jews pray for a good angel and get a rider in white with gold weapons leading the charge. Eleven thousand of Lysias's men die and he runs. Being, as the text puts it, not an unintelligent man, he works out these people cannot be beaten and starts negotiating. Next time on 2 Maccabees: four letters.
2MA 11:19-38	Four letters, back to back. Lysias says he will do what he can. The king tells Lysias to give the temple back and let them live by their own customs, mostly because quiet subjects are less hassle. The king tells the nation that anyone home by the thirtieth of Xanthicus gets an amnesty, their own food and their own laws, and that he is sending Menelaus to cheer them up, which is a bold pick. Then the Roman envoys write: we agree, send someone fast, we are heading to Antioch.
2MA 12:1-22	The peace lasts about a paragraph. The men of Joppa invite their Jewish neighbours onto boats with their wives and kids, sail out, and drown two hundred of them. Judas torches the harbour and the boats that night, then does Jamnia's too, and the fire is visible from Jerusalem. Some Arabs attack, lose, and immediately offer friendship and cattle, deal accepted. Caspin heckles him from the walls, gets stormed, and the lake next door runs red. Then Timotheus's hundred and twenty thousand men just panic. Next time on 2 Maccabees: Timotheus talks his way out.
2MA 12:23-45	Thirty thousand die in the chase. Timotheus gets caught and talks his way out by mentioning he is holding his captors' relatives, so they let him go. Carnion and Ephron fall. Scythopolis had been decent to its Jewish residents, so they get a thank you instead. Gorgias escapes only because a Thracian rider takes out the guy dragging him off by his cloak. Then they collect their own dead and find idols of Jamnia tucked under every tunic. Judas raises two thousand drachmas for a sin offering for them, because he expects them back.
2MA 13:1-26	Eupator and Lysias roll up with a hundred and ten thousand men, twenty-two elephants and three hundred scythed chariots. Menelaus comes along hoping to be handed the government and instead gets dropped into a fifty cubit tower full of ash at Beroea, the local execution for sacrilege, which the narrator thinks fits nicely given what he did to a holy altar. Judas night-raids the camp with the watchword Victory is God's and kills the biggest elephant. Then Philip revolts back home, the king suddenly loves peace, and leaves.
2MA 14:1-25	Demetrius lands at Tripolis, kills Antiochus and Lysias, takes the country. Alcimus, a disgraced ex high priest with no way back to the altar, shows up with a gold chaplet and a palm branch and tells the royal council that Judas is the only thing standing between the king and peace. So Nicanor, formerly in charge of the elephants, gets sent to kill him. Instead Nicanor hears how these people fight, opens talks, and ends up genuinely liking Judas, keeping him around and telling him to get married and settle down. He does. Next time on 2 Maccabees: Alcimus reads the treaty.
2MA 14:26-46	Alcimus brings Demetrius the treaty and calls it treason, and the order comes back: send Judas to Antioch in chains. Nicanor turns cold overnight, Judas reads the room and disappears. So Nicanor stands in the temple demanding the priests hand him over, and swears that otherwise he will flatten it and build a temple to Bacchus on the spot. Then five hundred soldiers surround Razis, an elder they called Father of the Jews, and he kills himself rather than be taken. The text spares no detail. There is no joke here.
2MA 15:1-19	Nicanor decides to attack on the Sabbath, and when the Jews with him object he asks whether there is honestly a Sovereign in heaven who ordered that, then announces he is a sovereign on earth. He does not pull it off. He also starts planning his victory monument in advance. Judas meanwhile rallies his men from the law and the prophets and tells them a dream: Onias the dead high priest praying for the people, and next to him Jeremiah, handing Judas a golden sword. They decide on a straight fight. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the sword gets used.
2MA 15:20-39	Elephants in position, cavalry on the flank, and Judas prays for the same kind of angel that dealt with Sennacherib. Nicanor comes on with trumpets and victory songs, Judas comes on praying, and thirty-five thousand of Nicanor's men die, Nicanor included, found dead in his armour. His head and the hand he waved at the temple end up hanging off the citadel, and the thirteenth of Adar becomes an annual holiday. Then the author signs off, hoping it was any good, and comparing writing to mixing wine with water, since neither works alone.
3JN 1:1-14	The elder writes to Gaius, thrilled that he's hosting travelling preachers who won't take cash from outsiders. Then the drama: Diotrephes, a man who loves being in charge, ignores the elder's letters entirely, talks trash about him, refuses to host anyone, and kicks out church members who do. The elder's plan is to show up and bring it up in front of everyone. Demetrius, meanwhile, gets a glowing reference. Full letter is basically church politics.
3MA 1:1-29	Philopater fights Antiochus at Raphia. An assassin gets into his tent and stabs the wrong guy, because Dositheus, a Jew who had quit the religion, had swapped in a body double. Arsinoe works the line in tears with her hair down, offering two minas of gold each, and it works. Ptolemy then goes on a temple tour, hits Jerusalem, sacrifices, and announces he would like to see inside the sanctuary. Told only the high priest goes in, once a year, he argues the rule obviously does not apply to him. The whole city pours into the streets.
3MA 2:1-17	Simon the high priest hits his knees and prays, and the prayer is well built. First the receipts: giants drowned, Sodom burned, Pharaoh rolled under the sea while Israel strolled across. Then the promise, that prayers made in this house get heard. Then he admits, no argument, that their own sins put them here. And the actual ask is not really rescue, it is reputation: do not let this place get defiled and hand the arrogant a story to brag about.
3MA 2:18-33	Prayer ends, God hears it, and the king gets shaken like a reed in the wind and dumped on the pavement, paralysed and unable to speak. His bodyguards haul him out. He recovers, repents of nothing, and leaves making threats. Back in Egypt he puts up a pillar: refuse to sacrifice and you are barred from your own temple, all Jews get registered as common people and branded with the ivy leaf of Dionysus, and resisters die. Join the cult and get Alexandrian citizenship. Most people just pay bribes instead.
3MA 3:1-16	The anger spreads from Alexandria to the countryside, with orders to round up the Jews and kill them. The accusation going around is that they keep everyone else from the ordinances of the law, which in practice means they eat differently and worship differently, therefore they must be antisocial and disloyal. Greek neighbours know better and cannot help, though some quietly promise to try. Then the royal letter, which opens with the king announcing that he is very well, his affairs are very well, and he was extremely generous to the temples. Next time on 3 Maccabees: the rest of the letter.
3MA 3:17-30	The letter goes on in the wounded tone of a man who was not allowed into a building. They only pretended to welcome him, they alone refuse to bow to kings, and when he generously offered them Alexandrian citizenship and initiation into the mysteries they said no, which he takes as proof they will betray him later. Therefore: every Jew, wife and children included, in iron chains, for a cruel death. Shelter one and your entire household is tortured. Turn one in and you get his property, two thousand drachmas, freedom and a crown.
3MA 4:1-21	The decree gets street parties. The deportation is bad enough that some of their enemies cry watching it: old men shoved along at speed, brides dragged out unveiled with dust in their perfumed hair, grooms in halters instead of garlands, everyone chained below deck with the planks blocking out the daylight. They are penned in the hippodrome in full view of everybody entering the city. Then the registration for execution runs forty days, dawn to dusk, and collapses, because there are too many Jews and the clerks have run out of paper and pens.
AMO 1:1-15	Amos is a sheep guy from Tekoa and he dates his own book by an earthquake. He opens with a lap around the neighbours: Damascus, Gaza, Tyre, Edom, Ammon. Same formula every time, three sins, no, make it four, and here comes fire on your palaces.
